),
and similarly by
\phi_{p}^{(0)}(t)
the flow at time
t

of the point
p
associated with (
3.1
) when
a=0
.
With a slight abuse of notation,
we will also write
\phi_{p}^{a}(t)=(u_{a}(t),v_{a}(t))
,
with
p=(u_{a}(0),v_{a}(0))
.

Let us start by proving that

\mathcal{M}^{a}\cap\big([0,u_{s}^{0}]\times[0,v_{s}^{0}]\big)\to\mathcal{M}^{0}\cap\big([0,u_{s}^{0}]\times[0,v_{s}^{0}]\big)\quad{\mbox{ as }}a\to 0.

(3.115)

For this, we claim that, for every
\varepsilon>0
,
if

(u_{a}(0))^{2}+(v_{a}(0))^{2}\geq\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{4}

(3.116)

and

\big|(u_{a}(t),v_{a}(t))-(u_{s}^{a},v_{s}^{a})\big|>\frac{\varepsilon}{2},

(3.117)

then

|\dot{u}_{a}(t)|^{2}+|\dot{v}_{a}(t)|^{2}>\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{C_{0}},

(3.118)

for some
C_{0}>0
, depending only on
\rho
and
c
.

Indeed, by (v) of
Theorem
3.2
and (
3.117
),
the trajectory
(u_{a}(t),v_{a}(t))
belongs to the set
[0,u_{s}^{a}]\times[0,v_{s}^{a}]\setminus B_{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}(u_{s}^{a},v_{s}^{a})\penalty\
.

Moreover, we claim that

1-ac-u_{a}(t)-v_{a}(t)\geq\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}{4},

(3.119)

for any
t>0
such that (
3.117
) is satisfied.
To prove this, we recall that
(u_{s}^{a},v_{s}^{a})
lies on the straight
line
\ell
given by
v=-u+1-ac

when
0<ac<1
(see (
3.21
)).
Clearly, there is no point of
the set
[0,u_{s}^{a}]\times[0,v_{s}^{a}]\setminus B_{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}(u_{s}^{a},v_{s}^{a})\penalty\

lying on
\ell
, and we notice that the points
in the set
[0,u_{s}^{a}]\times[0,v_{s}^{a}]\setminus B_{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}(u_{s}^{a},v_{s}^{a})\penalty\

with minimal distance from
\ell
are given by
p:=(u_{s}^{a}-\varepsilon/2,v_{s}^{a})
and
q:=(u_{s}^{a},v_{s}^{a}-\varepsilon/2)
.
Also, the distance of the point
p
from the straight line
\ell

is given by
\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\cdot\tan\frac{\pi}{4}=\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}{4}
.
Thus, the distance between
(u_{a}(t),v_{a}(t))

and the line
\ell
is greater than

\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}{4}
,
and this implies (
3.119
).

As a consequence of (
3.119
), we obtain that

(\dot{u}_{a}(t))^{2}=\big(u_{a}(t)(1-ac-u_{a}(t)-v_{a}(t))\big)^{2}>(u_{a}(t))^{2}\left(\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}{4}\right)^{2}

(3.120)

and that

\begin{split}(\dot{v}_{a}(t))^{2}\,=&\big(\rho v_{a}(t)(1-u_{a}(t)-v_{a}(t))-au_{a}(t)\big)^{2}\\
\geq&\left(\rho v_{a}(t)\left(ac+\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}{4}\right)-au_{a}(t)\right)^{2}.\end{split}

(3.121)

Now, if
u_{a}(t)\geq\rho cv_{a}(t)
, then from (
3.120
)
and (
3.116
)
we obtain that

\displaystyle(\dot{u}_{a}(t))^{2}+(\dot{v}_{a}(t))^{2}\geq(\dot{u}_{a}(t))^{2}>(u_{a}(t))^{2}\left(\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}{4}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\geq\frac{(u_{a}(t))^{2}}{2}\left(\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}{4}\right)^{2}+\frac{(\rho cv_{a}(t))^{2}}{2}\left(\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}{4}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\geq\min\{1,\rho^{2}c^{2}\}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{16}\big((u_{a}(t))^{2}+(v_{a}(t))^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\geq\min\{1,\rho^{2}c^{2}\}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{16}\big((u_{a}(0))^{2}+(v_{a}(0))^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\geq\min\{1,\rho^{2}c^{2}\}\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{64},

which proves (
3.118
) in this case.

If instead
u_{a}(t)<\rho cv_{a}(t)
, we use (
3.121
)
to see that

\displaystyle(\dot{u}_{a}(t))^{2}+(\dot{v}_{a}(t))^{2}\geq(\dot{v}_{a}(t))^{2}\geq\left(\rho v_{a}(t)\left(ac+\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}}{4}\right)-au_{a}(t)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\left(\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}\rho v_{a}(t)}{4}+a\big(\rho cv_{a}(t)-u_{a}(t)\big)\right)^{2}\geq\left(\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}\rho v_{a}(t)}{4}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\geq\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}\rho v_{a}(t)}{4}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{2}u_{a}(t)}{4c}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\geq\min\left\{\rho^{2},\frac{1}{c^{2}}\right\}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{16}\big((u_{a}(t))^{2}+(v_{a}(t))^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\geq\min\left\{\rho^{2},\frac{1}{c^{2}}\right\}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{16}\big((u_{a}(0))^{2}+(v_{a}(0))^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\geq\min\left\{\rho^{2},\frac{1}{c^{2}}\right\}\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{64},

which completes the proof of (
3.118
).

Now, for any
\eta>0
, we define

\mathcal{P}_{\eta}:=\left\{(u,v)\in[0,1]\times[0,1]\;{\mbox{ s.t. }}\;v=\frac{v_{s}^{0}-\eta^{\prime}}{(u_{s}^{0}+\eta^{\prime})^{\rho}}u^{\rho}\;{\mbox{ with }}|\eta^{\prime}|\leq\eta\right\}.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, we define

\eta(\varepsilon)
to be the smallest
\eta

for which
\mathcal{P}_{\eta}\supset B_{\varepsilon}(u_{s}^{0},v_{s}^{0})
.

(3.122)

We remark that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\eta(\varepsilon)=0.

(3.123)

Also, given
\delta>0
, we define a tubular
neighborhood
\mathcal{U}_{\delta}

of
\mathcal{M}^{0}
as

\mathcal{U}_{\delta}:=\bigcup_{q\in\mathcal{M}^{0}}B_{\delta}(q).

Furthermore, we define

\delta(\varepsilon)
the smallest
\delta
such that

\mathcal{U}_{\delta}\supset\mathcal{P}_{\eta(\varepsilon)}
.

(3.124)

Recalling (
3.123
), we have that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\delta(\varepsilon)=0.

(3.125)

We remark that, as
a\to 0
, the point
(u_{s}^{a},v_{s}^{a})
in (
3.11
),
which is a saddle point for the dynamics of (
3.1
)
when
ac<1
(recall Theorem
3.2
),
tends to the point
(u_{s}^{0},v_{s}^{0})
in (
3.14
), that belongs
to the line
v+u=1
, which is an equilibrium point for the dynamics of (
3.1
)
when
a=0
, according to Proposition
3.19
.

As a consequence, for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
a_{\varepsilon}>0

such that if
a\in(0,a_{\varepsilon})
,

|(u_{s}^{a},v_{s}^{a})-(u_{s}^{0},v_{s}^{0})|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{8}.

(3.126)

This gives that the intersection of
\mathcal{M}^{a}
with
B_{\varepsilon/2}(u_{s}^{0},v_{s}^{0})

is nonempty.

Furthermore, since
\gamma_{a}(0)=0
, in light of Proposition
3.8
,
we have that the intersection of
\mathcal{M}^{a}
with
B_{\varepsilon/2}

is nonempty. Hence, there exists
p_{\varepsilon,a}\in\mathcal{M}^{a}\cap\partial B_{\varepsilon/2}
.

We also notice that

\mathcal{M}^{a}=\phi_{p_{\varepsilon,a}}^{a}(\mathbb{R}).

(3.127)

In addition,

\phi_{p_{\varepsilon,a}}^{a}\big((-\infty,0]\big)\subset B_{\varepsilon/2}.

(3.128)

Also, since the origin belongs to
\mathcal{M}^{0}
, we have that
B_{\varepsilon/2}\subset\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}
. From
this and (
3.128
), we deduce that

\phi_{p_{\varepsilon,a}}^{a}\big((-\infty,0]\big)\subset\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}.

(3.129)

Now, we let
C_{0}
be as in (
3.118
) and
we claim that there exists
t_{\varepsilon,a}\in(0,3\sqrt{C_{0}}\varepsilon^{-2})

such that

\phi_{p_{\varepsilon,a}}^{a}(t_{\varepsilon,a})\in\partial B_{3\varepsilon/4}(u_{s}^{0},v_{s}^{0}).

(3.130)

To check this, we argue by contradiction and we suppose that

\phi_{p_{\varepsilon,a}}^{a}\big((0,3\sqrt{C_{0}}\varepsilon^{-2})\big)\cap B_{3\varepsilon/4}(u_{s}^{0},v_{s}^{0})=\varnothing.

Then, for every
t\in(0,3\sqrt{C_{0}}\varepsilon^{-2})
,
recalling also (
3.126
),

\big|\phi_{p_{\varepsilon,a}}^{a}(t)-(u_{s}^{a},v_{s}^{a})\big|\geq\big|\phi_{p_{\varepsilon,a}}^{a}(t)-(u_{s}^{0},v_{s}^{0})\big|-\big|(u_{s}^{a},v_{s}^{a})-(u_{s}^{0},v_{s}^{0})\big|\geq\frac{3\varepsilon}{4}-\frac{\varepsilon}{8}>\frac{\varepsilon}{2},

and consequently (
3.117
) is satisfied for every
t\in(0,3\sqrt{C_{0}}\varepsilon^{-2})
.

Moreover,
we observe that
p_{\varepsilon,a}
satisfies (
3.116
),
and therefore, by (
3.118
),

|\dot{u}_{a}(t)|^{2}+|\dot{v}_{a}(t)|^{2}>\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{C_{0}},

for all
t\in(0,3\sqrt{C_{0}}\varepsilon^{-2})
,
where we used the notation
{\phi}_{p_{\varepsilon,a}}^{a}(t)=(u_{a}(t),v_{a}(t))
, being
p_{\varepsilon,a}=(u_{a}(0),v_{a}(0))
.
As a result,

\big(\dot{u}_{a}(t)+\dot{v}_{a}(t)\big)^{2}>\frac{\varepsilon^{4}}{C_{0}},

and thus

\dot{u}_{a}(t)+\dot{v}_{a}(t)>\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\sqrt{C_{0}}}.

This leads to

\displaystyle u_{a}\left(\frac{3\sqrt{C_{0}}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)+v_{a}\left(\frac{3\sqrt{C_{0}}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)=u_{a}(0)+v_{a}(0)+\int_{0}^{\frac{3\sqrt{C_{0}}}{\varepsilon^{2}}}\big(\dot{u}_{a}(t)+\dot{v}_{a}(t)\big)\,dt

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\geq u_{a}(0)+v_{a}(0)+\int_{0}^{\frac{3\sqrt{C_{0}}}{\varepsilon^{2}}}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\sqrt{C_{0}}}\,dt=u_{a}(0)+v_{a}(0)+3\geq 3,

which forces the trajectory to exit the region
[0,1]\times[0,1]
.
This is against the assumption that
p_{\varepsilon,a}\in\mathcal{M}^{a}
,
and therefore the proof of (
3.130
) is complete.

In light of (
3.130
), we can set
q_{\varepsilon,a}:=\phi_{p_{\varepsilon,a}}^{a}(t_{\varepsilon,a})
,
and we deduce from (
3.122
) that
q_{\varepsilon,a}\in\mathcal{P}_{\eta(\varepsilon)}
.
We also observe that the set
\mathcal{P}_{\eta}
is invariant for the
flow with
a=0
, thanks to (
3.109
). These observations
give that
\phi_{q_{\varepsilon,a}}^{0}(t)\in\mathcal{P}_{\eta(\varepsilon)}

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

As a result, using (
3.124
), we conclude that

\phi_{q_{\varepsilon,a}}^{0}(t)\in\mathcal{U}_{\delta(\varepsilon)}\quad{\mbox{ for all }}t\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.131)

In addition, by the continuous dependence of the flow
on the parameter
a
(see e.g. Section 2.4
in
[
60
]
,
or Theorem 2.4.2 in
[
63
]
),

\big|\phi_{q_{\varepsilon,a}}^{0}(t)-\phi_{q_{\varepsilon,a}}^{a}(t)\big|<\varepsilon,

for all
t\in[-3\sqrt{C_{0}}\varepsilon^{-2},0]
, provided that
a

is sufficiently small, possibly in dependence of
\varepsilon
.
This fact and (
3.131
) entail that

\phi_{q_{\varepsilon,a}}^{a}(t)\in\mathcal{U}_{\delta(\varepsilon)+\varepsilon}\quad{\mbox{ for all }}t\in[-3\sqrt{C_{0}}\varepsilon^{-2},0].

In particular, for all
t\in[0,t_{\varepsilon,a}]
,

\phi_{p_{\varepsilon,a}}^{a}(t)=\phi_{q_{\varepsilon,a}}^{a}(t-t_{\varepsilon,a})\in\mathcal{U}_{\delta(\varepsilon)+\varepsilon}.

(3.132)

We now claim that
for all
t\geq t_{\varepsilon,a}
,

\phi_{p_{\varepsilon,a}}^{a}(t)\subset B_{\varepsilon}(u_{s}^{a},v_{s}^{a}).

(3.133)

Indeed, this is true when
t=t_{\varepsilon,a}
thanks to (
3.126
)
and (
3.130
). Hence, since
the trajectory
\phi_{p_{\varepsilon,a}}^{a}(t)

is contained in the domain where
\dot{u}\geq 0
and
\dot{v}\geq 0
,
thanks to (
3.27
), we deduce that (
3.133
) holds true.

From (
3.126
) and (
3.133
), we conclude that

\phi_{p_{\varepsilon,a}}^{a}(t)\subset B_{2\varepsilon}(u_{s}^{0},v_{s}^{0}),

for all
t\geq t_{\varepsilon,a}
.

Using this, (
3.129
)
and (
3.132
), we obtain that

\phi_{p_{\varepsilon,a}}^{a}(\mathbb{R})\subset\mathcal{U}_{\delta(\varepsilon)+2\varepsilon}.

This and (
3.125
) give that (
3.115
) is satisfied, as desired.

One can also show that

\mathcal{M}^{a}\cap\big([u_{s}^{0},u_{\mathcal{M}}^{0}]\times[v_{s}^{0},v_{\mathcal{M}}^{0}]\big)\to\mathcal{M}^{0}\cap\big([u_{s}^{0},u_{\mathcal{M}}^{0}]\times[v_{s}^{0},v_{\mathcal{M}}^{0}]\big)\quad{\mbox{ as }}a\to 0.

(3.134)

The proof of (
3.134
) is similar to that of (
3.115
),
just replacing
p_{\varepsilon,a}
with
(u_{\mathcal{M}}^{a},v_{\mathcal{M}}^{a})
(in this case
the analysis near the origin is simply omitted since the trajectory
has only one limit point).

With (
3.115
) and (
3.134
)
the proof of Lemma
3.21
is thereby complete.
∎

Now we are ready to give the proof of Proposition
3.20
:

Proof of Proposition
3.20
.

(i)
We call
\mathcal{G}
the right-hand-side of (
3.112
), that is

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\big\{(u,v)\in[0,1]\times[0,1]\;{\mbox{ s.t. }}\;v<\gamma_{0}(u)\,\text{ if }\,u\in[0,u_{\mathcal{M}}^{0}]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad{\mbox{and }}\;v\leq 1\,\text{ if }\,u\in(u_{\mathcal{M}}^{0},1]\big\},

and we aim at proving that
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{0}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{G}}
.

For this, we observe that, by Lemma
3.21
,

\gamma_{a}(u)
converges to
\gamma_{0}(u)
pointwise as
a\to 0
.
In particular,
u_{\mathcal{M}}^{a}\to u_{\mathcal{M}}^{0}
as
a\to 0
.

Also, recalling (
3.108
),
we notice that if
u_{\mathcal{M}}^{0}=u_{s}^{0}/(v_{s}^{0})^{\frac{1}{\rho}}<1
,
then
\gamma_{0}(u_{\mathcal{M}}^{0})=1
,
otherwise if
u_{\mathcal{M}}^{0}=1

then
\gamma_{0}(u_{\mathcal{M}}^{0})<1
, being
\gamma_{0}(u)
strictly
monotone increasing.

Furthermore, thanks to Proposition
3.16
,
we know that
he set
\mathcal{E}(a)
is bounded from above by the graph of the function
\gamma_{a}(u)
for
u\in[0,u_{\mathcal{M}}^{a}]
and from the
straight line
v=1
for
u\in(u_{\mathcal{M}}^{a},1]
(that is non empty for
u_{\mathcal{M}}^{a}<1
).

Now we claim that, for all
a^{\prime}>0
,

\mathcal{G}\subseteq\underset{0<a<a^{\prime}}{\bigcup}\mathcal{E}(a).

(3.135)

To show this, we take a point
(u,v)\in\mathcal{G}
.
Hence, in light of the considerations
above, we have that
(u,v)\in\mathcal{E}(a)
for any
a
sufficiently small,
which proves (
3.135
).

From (
3.135
), we deduce that

\mathcal{G}\subseteq\underset{a^{\prime}>0}{\bigcap}\,\underset{0<a<a^{\prime}}{\bigcup}\mathcal{E}(a).

(3.136)

Now we show that

\underset{a^{\prime}>0}{\bigcap}\,\underset{0<a<a^{\prime}}{\bigcup}\mathcal{E}(a)\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{G}}.

(3.137)

For this, we take

(\hat{u},\hat{v})\in\underset{a^{\prime}>0}{\bigcap}\,\underset{0<a<a^{\prime}}{\bigcup}\mathcal{E}(a),

then it must hold that for every
a^{\prime}>0

there exists
a<a^{\prime}
such that
(\hat{u},\hat{v})\in\mathcal{E}(a)
,
namely
\hat{v}<\gamma_{a}(\hat{u})
if
\hat{u}\in[0,u_{\mathcal{M}}^{a}]
and
\hat{v}\leq 1
if
\hat{u}\in(u_{\mathcal{M}}^{a},1]
.
Thus, by the pointwise convergence,
we have that
\hat{v}\leq\gamma_{0}(\hat{u})\penalty\
if
\hat{u}\in[0,u_{\mathcal{M}}^{0}]
and
\hat{v}\leq 1
if
\hat{u}\in(u_{\mathcal{M}}^{0},1]
, which proves (
3.137
).

From (
3.136
) and (
3.137
),
we conclude that

\mathcal{G}\subseteq\underset{a^{\prime}>0}{\bigcap}\,\underset{0<a<a^{\prime}}{\bigcup}\mathcal{E}(a)=\mathcal{E}_{0}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{G}},

as desired.

(ii)
Since we deal with the limit case as
a\to+\infty
, from now on
we suppose from now on that
ac>1
.
We fix
\varepsilon>0
and we consider the set

\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon^{+}}:=\left\{(u,v)\in[0,1]\times[0,1]\;{\mbox{ s.t. }}\;v>u\left(\frac{1}{c}+\varepsilon\right)\right\}.

We claim that

\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon^{+}}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(a)

(3.138)

for
a
big enough, possibly in dependence of
\varepsilon
.
For this,
we first analyze the component of the velocity in the inward normal directions
along the boundary of
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon^{+}}
.
On the side
\{0\}\times[0,1]
,
the trajectories cannot cross the boundary thanks to Proposition
3.1
, and the same happens for the sides
[0,1]\times\{1\}
and
\{1\}\times[\varepsilon+1/c,1]
.

Hence, it remains to check the sign of the normal derivative along the
side given by the straight line
v-u(\varepsilon+1/c)=0
.
We compute

\displaystyle(\dot{u},\dot{v})\cdot\left(-\left(\varepsilon+\frac{1}{c}\right),1\right)=\dot{v}-\dot{u}\left(\varepsilon+\frac{1}{c}\right)

\displaystyle\quad={\rho}v(1-u-v)-au-\left(\varepsilon+\frac{1}{c}\right)u(1-u-v)+\left(\varepsilon+\frac{1}{c}\right)acu

\displaystyle\quad=\Bigg[{\rho}v-\left(\varepsilon+\frac{1}{c}\right)u\Bigg](1-u-v)+\varepsilon acu.

Thus, by using that
v-u(\varepsilon+1/c)=0
, we obtain that

\displaystyle(\dot{u},\dot{v})\cdot\left(-\left(\varepsilon+\frac{1}{c}\right),1\right)\geq u\left[a\varepsilon c+({\rho}-1)(1-u-v)\left(\varepsilon+\frac{1}{c}\right)\right].

Notice that
u\leq 1
and
|1-u-v|\leq 2
, and therefore

(\dot{u},\dot{v})\cdot\left(-\left(\varepsilon+\frac{1}{c}\right),1\right)\geq u\left[a\varepsilon c-2({\rho}+1)\left(\varepsilon+\frac{1}{c}\right)\right].

Accordingly, the normal velocity is positive for
a\geq{a}_{1}
, where

{a}_{1}:=2({\rho}+1)\left(\varepsilon+\frac{1}{c}\right)\frac{1}{\varepsilon c}.

These considerations, together with the fact that there are no cycles
in
[0,1]\times[0,1]
and the
Poincaré-Bendixson Theorem (see e.g.
[
113
]
)
give that the
\omega
-limit set of any trajectory starting
in the interior of
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon^{+}}

can be either an equilibrium or a union of (finitely many)
equilibria and non-closed orbits connecting these equilibria.

We remark that

the
\omega
-limit set of any trajectory cannot
be the equilibrium
(0,0)
.

(3.139)

Indeed, if the
\omega
-limit of a trajectory
were
(0,0)
, then this trajectory must lie on the stable manifold of
(0,0)
,
and moreover it must be contained in
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon^{+}}
,
since no trajectory can exit
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon^{+}}
.
On the other hand, by Proposition
3.8
,
we have that at
u=0
the stable manifold is tangent to the
line

v=\frac{a}{\rho-1+ac}u=\frac{1}{\frac{\rho-1}{a}+c}u.

Now, if we take
a
sufficiently large, this line lies
below the line
v=u(1/c+\varepsilon)
, thus providing a contradiction.
Hence, the proof of (
3.139
) is complete.

Accordingly, since
(0,1)
is a sink, the only possibility is that
the
\omega
-limit set of any trajectory starting
in the interior of
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon^{+}}
is the equilibrium
(0,1)
.
Namely, we have established (
3.138
).

As a consequence of (
3.138
), we deduce that for every
\varepsilon>0

there exists
a_{\varepsilon}>0
such that

\underset{a\geq a_{\varepsilon}}{\bigcup}\mathcal{E}(a)\subseteq\left\{(u,v)\in[0,1]\times[0,1]\;{\mbox{ s.t. }}\;v\leq u\left(\frac{1}{c}+\varepsilon\right)\right\}.

(3.140)

In addition,

\begin{split}&\underset{\varepsilon>0}{\bigcap}\left\{(u,v)\in[0,1]\times[0,1]\;{\mbox{ s.t. }}\;v\leq u\left(\frac{1}{c}+\varepsilon\right)\right\}\\
&\qquad=\left\{(u,v)\in[0,1]\times[0,1]\;{\mbox{ s.t. }}\;v\leq\frac{u}{c}\right\}=\overline{\mathcal{S}_{c}}.\end{split}

This and (
3.140
) entail that

\underset{a^{\prime}>0}{\bigcap}\,\underset{a>a^{\prime}}{\bigcup}\mathcal{E}(a)\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{S}_{c}},

which implies the second inclusion in (
3.113
).

Now, to show the first inclusion in (
3.113
),
for every
\varepsilon\in(0,1/c)
we consider the set

\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon^{-}}:=\left\{(u,v)\in[0,1]\times[0,1]\;{\mbox{ s.t. }}\;v<u\left(\frac{1}{c}-\varepsilon\right)\right\}.

We claim that, for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1/c)
,

\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon^{-}}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{\infty}.

(3.141)

For this, we first show that if
a
is sufficiently large, possibly
in dependence of
\varepsilon
,

\begin{split}&{\mbox{every trajectory starting in the interior of\penalty\ $\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon^{-}}$}}\\
&{\mbox{can exit\penalty\ $\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon^{-}}$ from the side\penalty\ $[0,1]\times\{0\}$.}}\end{split}

(3.142)

Indeed,
on the side
\{1\}\times[0,1]
the trajectory cannot exit the set,
thanks to Proposition
3.1
.
On the side given by
v-(-\varepsilon+1/c)u=0
, the component of the velocity
in the direction of the
outward normal is

\displaystyle(\dot{u},\dot{v})\cdot\left(-\left(\frac{1}{c}-\varepsilon\right),1\right)=\dot{v}-\dot{u}\left(\frac{1}{c}-\varepsilon\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\rho v(1-u-v)-au-\left(\frac{1}{c}-\varepsilon\right)u(1-u-v)+\left(\frac{1}{c}-\varepsilon\right)acu

\displaystyle\qquad=u\left[\left(\frac{1}{c}-\varepsilon\right)(\rho-1)(1-u-v)-\varepsilon ac\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\leq u\left[2\left(\frac{1}{c}-\varepsilon\right)(\rho+1)-\varepsilon ac\right],

which is negative if
a\geq{a}_{2}
, with

{a}_{2}:=2\left(\frac{1}{c}-\varepsilon\right)\left(\rho+1\right)\frac{1}{\varepsilon c}.

Hence, if
(u(0),v(0))\in\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon^{-}}
, then
either
T_{s}(u(0),v(0))<\infty
or
(u(t),v(t))\in\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon^{-}}

for all
t\geq 0
, where the notation in (
3.8
) has been used.
We also notice that,
for
a>1/c
, the points
(0,1)
and
(0,0)
are the only equilibria
of the system, and there are no cycles.
We have that
(0,1)\notin\overline{\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon^{-}}}

and
(0,0)\in\overline{\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon^{-}}}
, thus if

(u(t),v(t))\in\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon^{-}}
for all
t\geq 0

(3.143)

then

(u(t),v(t))\to(0,0).

(3.144)

On the other hand, by Proposition
3.8
,
we have that at
u=0
the stable manifold is tangent to the
line

v=\frac{a}{\rho-1+ac}u=\frac{1}{\frac{\rho-1}{a}+c}u,

and, if we take
a
large enough, this line lies
above the line
v=u(1/c-\varepsilon)
. This says that, for sufficiently large
t
,
the trajectory must lie outside
\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon^{-}}
,
and this is in contradiction with (
3.143
).

As a result of these considerations, we conclude
that if
(u(0),v(0))\in\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon^{-}}
then
T_{s}(u(0),v(0))<\infty
,
which implies (
3.142
).

As a consequence of (
3.142
), we obtain that for every
\varepsilon\in(0,1/c)

there exists
a_{\varepsilon}>0
such that

\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon^{-}}\subseteq\underset{a\geq a_{\varepsilon}}{\bigcap}\mathcal{E}(a).

In particular for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1/c)
it holds that

\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon^{-}}\subseteq\underset{a^{\prime}>0}{\bigcap}\,\underset{a>a^{\prime}}{\bigcup}\mathcal{E}(a)=\mathcal{E}_{\infty},

which proves (
3.141
), as desired.

Then, the first inclusion in (
3.113
) plainly follows
from (
3.141
).
∎

3.4
Analysis of the strategies for the first population

The main theorems on the winning strategy have
been stated in Subsection
3.1.4
.
In particular, Theorem
3.3
gives the characterization of the set
of points that have a winning strategy
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}

in (
3.13
),
and Theorem
3.4
establishes the non equivalence of constant
and non-constant strategies when
\rho\neq 1

(and their equivalence when
\rho=1
).
Nonetheless, in Theorem
3.5
we state that
Heaviside functions are enough to construct a
winning strategy for every point in
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

In the following subsections we will give the proofs of these results.

3.4.1
Construction of winning non-constant strategies

We want to put in light the construction of non-constant winning
strategies for the points for which constant strategies fail.

For this, we recall the notation introduced in (
3.14
),
(
3.18
) and (
3.108
), and we have the following statement:

Proposition 3.22
.

Let
M>1
. Then we have:

1.

For
\rho<1
, let
(u_{0},v_{0})
be a point of the set

\mathcal{P}:=\left\{(u,v)\in[0,1]\times[0,1]\;{\mbox{ s.t. }}\;u\in[u_{s}^{0},1],\ \gamma_{0}(u)\leq v<\frac{u}{c}+\frac{1-\rho}{1+\rho c}\right\}.

(3.145)

Then there exist
a^{*}>M
,
a_{*}<\frac{1}{M}
,
and
T\geq 0
, depending on
(u_{0},v_{0})
,
c
, and
\rho
, such that
the Heaviside strategy defined by

a(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}a^{*},&{\mbox{ if }}t<T,\\
a_{*},&{\mbox{ if }}t\geq T,\end{array}\right.

(3.146)

belongs to
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

2.

For
\rho>1
, let
(u_{0},v_{0})
be a point of the set

\mathcal{Q}:=\left\{(u,v)\in[0,1]\times[0,1]\;{\mbox{ s.t. }}\;u\in[u_{\infty},1],\ \frac{u}{c}\leq v<\zeta(u)\right\}.

(3.147)

Then there exist
a^{*}>M
,
a_{*}<\frac{1}{M}
, and
T\geq 0
, depending on
(u_{0},v_{0})
,
c
, and
\rho
, such that the Heaviside strategy
defined by

a(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}a_{*},&{\mbox{ if }}t<T,\\
a^{*},&{\mbox{ if }}t\geq T,\end{array}\right.

belongs to
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

Proof.

We start by proving the first claim in Proposition
3.22
.
To this aim, we take
(\bar{u},\bar{v})\in\mathcal{P}
, and we observe that

\bar{v}-\frac{\bar{u}}{c}<\frac{1-\rho}{1+\rho c}=v_{s}^{0}-\frac{u_{s}^{0}}{c}.

Therefore, there exists
\xi>0
such that

\xi<\frac{v_{s}^{0}-\bar{v}-\frac{1}{c}(u_{s}^{0}-\bar{u})}{\bar{u}-u_{s}^{0}}.

Hence, setting

v_{S}:=\left(\frac{1}{c}-\xi\right)(u_{s}^{0}-\bar{u})+\bar{v},

(3.148)

we see that

v_{S}<v_{s}^{0}.

(3.149)

Now, we want to show that there exists
a^{*}>0
such that, for any
a>a^{*}
and
u>u_{s}^{0}
, we have that

\frac{\dot{v}}{\dot{u}}>\frac{1}{c}-\xi.

(3.150)

To prove this, we first notice that

if
a>\displaystyle\frac{2}{c}
, then
\dot{u}\leq-u<0
.

(3.151)

Moreover, we set

a_{1}:=\frac{1+\rho c}{4c},\penalty\

and we claim that,

if
a>a_{1}
and
u>u_{s}^{0}
, then
\dot{v}<0
.

(3.152)

Indeed, we recall that the function
\sigma

defined in (
3.26
) represents the points
in
[0,1]\times[0,1]
where
\dot{v}=0

and separates the points where
\dot{v}>0
, which lie on the left of
the curve described by
\sigma
, from the points where
\dot{v}<0
, which lie on the right of
the curve described by
\sigma
.

Therefore, in order to show (
3.152
), it is sufficient to prove that
the curve described by
\sigma

is contained in
\{u\leq u_{s}^{0}\}
whenever
a>a_{1}
. For this, one computes that, if
u=\sigma(v)

and
a>a_{1}
, then

\displaystyle u-u_{s}^{0}=\sigma(v)-\frac{\rho c}{1+\rho c}=1-\frac{\rho v^{2}+a}{\rho v+a}-\frac{\rho c}{1+\rho c}

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{\rho v-\rho v^{2}}{\rho v+a}-\frac{\rho c}{1+\rho c}=\frac{\rho v(1-v)}{\rho v+a}-\frac{\rho c}{1+\rho c}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{\rho}{4(\rho v+a)}-\frac{\rho c}{1+\rho c}\leq\frac{\rho}{4a}-\frac{\rho c}{1+\rho c}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{\rho}{4a_{1}}-\frac{\rho c}{1+\rho c}\leq 0.

This completes the proof of (
3.152
).

Now we define

a_{2}:=\left(\rho+\frac{1}{c}+\xi\right)\frac{2}{u_{s}^{0}c\xi}.

and we claim that

{\mbox{if\penalty\ $a>a_{2}$ and\penalty\ $u>u_{s}^{0}$, then }}\dot{v}<\left(\frac{1}{c}-\xi\right)\dot{u}.

(3.153)

Indeed, under the assumptions of (
3.153
),
we deduce that

\displaystyle\dot{v}-\left(\frac{1}{c}-\xi\right)\dot{u}=\rho v(1-u-v)-au-\left(\frac{1}{c}-\xi\right)\Big(u(1-u-v)-acu\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad=(1-u-v)\left(\rho v-\left(\frac{1}{c}-\xi\right)u\right)-ac\xi u\leq 2\left(\rho v+\frac{u}{c}+\xi u\right)-ac\xi u

\displaystyle\qquad<2\left(\rho+\frac{1}{c}+\xi\right)-a_{2}\,c\xi{u_{s}^{0}}=0,

and this establishes the claim in (
3.153
).

Then, choosing

a^{*}:=\max\left\{\displaystyle\frac{2}{c},a_{1},a_{2},M\right\},

we can exploit (
3.151
), (
3.152
) and (
3.153
)
to deduce (
3.150
), as desired.

Now we claim that, for any
a>a^{*}
, there exists
T\geq 0

such that the trajectory
(u(t),v(t))
starting from
(\bar{u},\bar{v})
satisfies

u(T)=u_{s}^{0}
and
v(T)<v_{S}
.

(3.154)

Indeed, we define
T\geq 0
to be the first time for which
u(T)=u_{s}^{0}
.
This is a fair definition, since
u(0)=\bar{u}\geq u_{s}^{0}

and
\dot{u}
is negative, and bounded away from zero
till
u\geq u_{s}^{0}
, thanks to (
3.151
).
Then, we see that

\displaystyle v(T)=\bar{v}+\int_{0}^{T}\dot{v}(t)\,dt<\bar{v}+\int_{0}^{T}\left(\frac{1}{c}-\xi\right)\,\dot{u}(t)\,dt=\bar{v}+\left(\frac{1}{c}-\xi\right)(u(T)-u(0))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\bar{v}+\left(\frac{1}{c}-\xi\right)(u_{s}^{0}-\bar{u})=v_{S},

thanks to (
3.148
) and (
3.150
), and this establishes (
3.154
).

Now we observe that

v(T)<v_{S}<v_{s}^{0}=\gamma_{0}(u_{s}^{0})=\gamma_{0}(u(T))

due to (
3.149
)
and (
3.154
)

As a result, recalling Lemma
3.21
, we can choose
a_{*}<1/M

such that

v(T)<\gamma_{a_{*}}(u(T)).

Accordingly, by Proposition
3.16
,
we obtain that
(u(T),v(T))\in{\mathcal{E}}(a_{*})
.
Hence, applying the strategy
in (
3.146
),
we accomplish the desired result and complete the proof of the first claim
in Proposition
3.22
.

Now we focus on the proof of the second claim in Proposition
3.22
.
For this, let

(u_{0},v_{0})\in\mathcal{Q},

(3.155)

and consider the trajectory
(u_{0}(t),v_{0}(t))

starting from
(u_{0},v_{0})
for the strategy
a=0
. In light of formula (
3.106
)
of Proposition
3.19
, we have that

\begin{split}&{\mbox{the trajectory\penalty\ $(u_{0}(t),v_{0}(t))$
converges}}\\
&{\mbox{to a point of the form\penalty\ $(u_{F},1-u_{F})$ as\penalty\ $t\to+\infty$.}}\end{split}

(3.156)

We define

v_{F}:=1-u_{F},\quad v_{\infty}:=1-u_{\infty}=\frac{1}{c+1},

(3.157)

where the last equality can be checked starting from the value of
u_{\infty}
given in (
3.18
).
Using the definition of
\zeta
in (
3.18
) and the information in (
3.109
),
we also notice that the curve given by
v=\zeta(u)
is a trajectory for
a=0
. Moreover

\zeta(u_{\infty})=\frac{1}{c(u_{\infty})^{\rho-1}}u_{\infty}^{\rho}=\frac{c}{c(c+1)}=v_{\infty}

and, recalling (
3.157
) and formula (
3.106
) of Proposition
3.19
, we get that
the graph of
\zeta
is a trajectory for
a=0
that converges to
(u_{\infty},1-u_{\infty})
as
t\to+\infty
.

Also, by (
3.155
), we have that
v_{0}<\zeta(u_{0})
. Thus, since by Cauchy’s uniqueness result for ODEs, two orbits never intersect, we have that

the orbit
(u_{0}(t),v_{0}(t))
must lie below the graph of
\zeta
.

(3.158)

Since both
(u_{F},v_{F})
and
(u_{\infty},v_{\infty})
belong to the line given by
v=1-u
, from (
3.158
) we get that

u_{\infty}<u_{F}

(3.159)

and

v_{\infty}>v_{F}.

(3.160)

Thanks to (
3.159
) and (
3.160
) and recalling the values of
u_{\infty}
from (
3.18
) and of
v_{\infty}
from (
3.157
), we get that

v_{F}<v_{\infty}=\frac{u_{\infty}}{c}<\frac{u_{F}}{c}.

(3.161)

As a consequence, since the inequality in (
3.161
) is strict,
we find that there exists
T^{\prime}>0

such that

v_{0}(T^{\prime})<\frac{u_{0}(T^{\prime})}{c}.

(3.162)

Moreover, since
\dot{u}<0
for
v>1-u
and
a=0
, we get that

u_{0}(t)
is decreasing in
t
, and therefore
u_{F}<u_{0}(T^{\prime})<u_{0}
.

By the strict inequality in (
3.162
), and
claim (ii) in Proposition
3.20
,
we have that
(u_{0}(T^{\prime}),v_{0}(T^{\prime}))\in\mathcal{E}_{\infty}
, where
\mathcal{E}_{\infty}
is defined in (
3.111
).
In particular, we have that
(u_{0}(T^{\prime}),v_{0}(T^{\prime}))\in\underset{a>a^{\prime}}{\bigcup}\mathcal{E}(a)
, for every
a^{\prime}>0
.
Consequently, there exists
a^{*}>M
such that
(u_{0}(T^{\prime}),v_{0}(T^{\prime}))\in\mathcal{E}(a^{*})
. Therefore, applying the strategy

a(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}0,&t<T^{\prime},\\
a^{*},&t\geq T,\end{array}\right.

we reach the victory.
∎

3.4.2
Proof of Theorem
3.3

To avoid repeating passages in the proofs of Theorems
3.3
and
3.4
, we first state and prove the following lemma:

Lemma 3.23
.

If
\rho=1
, then for all
a>0
we have
\mathcal{E}(a)=\mathcal{S}_{c}
, where
\mathcal{S}_{c}
was defined in (
3.114
).

Proof.

Let
(u(t),v(t))
be a trajectory starting at a point
in
[0,1]\times[0,1]
.
For any
a>0
, we consider the function

\mu(t):=\frac{\displaystyle v\left(\frac{t}{a}\right)}{\displaystyle u\left(\frac{t}{a}\right)}.

Notice that

(u(0),v(0))\in\mathcal{E}(a){\mbox{ if and only if there exists\penalty\ $T>0$ such that }}\mu(T)=0.

(3.163)

In addition, we observe that

\begin{split}\dot{\mu}(t)\,&=\frac{\displaystyle\dot{v}\left(\frac{t}{a}\right)u\left(\frac{t}{a}\right)-v\left(\frac{t}{a}\right)\dot{u}\left(\frac{t}{a}\right)}{\displaystyle au^{2}\left(\frac{t}{a}\right)}\\
&=\frac{\displaystyle-u^{2}\left(\frac{t}{a}\right)+cu\left(\frac{t}{a}\right)v\left(\frac{t}{a}\right)}{\displaystyle u^{2}\left(\frac{t}{a}\right)}\\
&=c\mu(t)-1.\end{split}

(3.164)

The equation in (
3.164
) is integrable and leads to

\mu(t)=\frac{e^{ct}\left(c\mu(0)-1\right)+1}{c}.

From this and (
3.163
), we deduce that

(u(0),v(0))\in\mathcal{E}(a){\mbox{ if and only if }}c\mu(0)-1<0.

This leads to

(u(0),v(0))\in\mathcal{E}(a){\mbox{ if and only if }}\,\frac{v(0)}{u(0)}<\frac{1}{c},

which, recalling the definition of
\mathcal{S}_{c}

in (
3.114
), ends the proof.
∎

Now we provide the proof of Theorem
3.3
, exploiting the result obtained
in Section
3.4.1
.

Proof of Theorem
3.3
.

(i)
Let
\rho=1
. For the sake of simplicity, we suppose that
c\geq 1
, and therefore the second line in (
3.15
) is not present
(the proof of (
3.15
) when
c<1
is similar, but one has to take into account also the set
(c,1]\times[0,1]
and show that it is contained
in
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
by checking the sign of the component
of the velocity field in the normal direction).

We claim that

\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{S}_{c}
,

(3.165)

where
\mathcal{S}_{c}
was
defined in (
3.114
)
(incidentally,
\mathcal{S}_{c}

is precisely the right-hand-side of equation (
3.15
)).

From Lemma
3.23
we have that for
\rho=1
and
a>0
it holds
\mathcal{S}_{c}=\mathcal{E}(a)\subset\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
. Thus, to show (
3.165
) we just need to check that

\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{c},

(3.166)

which is equivalent to

\mathcal{S}_{c}^{C}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}^{C},

(3.167)

where the superscript
C
denotes the complement of the set in the topology of
[0,1]\times[0,1]
.

First, by definition we have that

\mathcal{S}_{c}^{C}\cap((0,1]\times\{0\})=\varnothing.

(3.168)

Now, we analyze the behavior of the trajectories at
\partial\mathcal{S}_{c}^{C}
. By Proposition
3.1
, no trajectory can exit
\mathcal{S}_{c}^{C}
from a point on
\partial([0,1]\times[0,1])\setminus((0,1]\times\{0\})
. Moreover,

\partial\mathcal{S}_{c}^{C}\cap((0,1]\times\{0\})=\varnothing

thanks to (
3.168
) and
the fact that
\mathcal{S}_{c}^{C}
is closed in the topology of
[0,1]\times[0,1]
.
Hence,

no trajectory can exit
\mathcal{S}_{c}^{C}
from a point on
\partial([0,1]\times[0,1])
.

(3.169)

Furthermore, it holds that

\partial\mathcal{S}_{c}^{C}\cap\big((0,1)\times(0,1)\big)=\left\{(u,v)\in(0,1)\times(0,1)\;{\mbox{ s.t. }}\;v=\frac{u}{c}\right\}.

The velocity of a trajectory starting on the line
v=\frac{u}{c}
in the orthogonal direction pointing inward
\mathcal{S}_{c}^{C}
is

(\dot{u},\dot{v})\cdot\frac{(-1,c)}{\sqrt{c^{2}+1}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{c^{2}+1}}(cv-u)(1-u-v)=0,

the last equality coming from the fact that
cv=u
on
\partial\mathcal{S}_{c}^{C}\cap\big((0,1)\times(0,1)\big)
.
This means that

no trajectory can exit
\mathcal{S}_{c}^{C}
from a point on the line
v=\frac{u}{c}
.

(3.170)

From (
3.169
) and (
3.170
), we get that no trajectory exits
\mathcal{S}_{c}^{C}
. Then, by (
3.168
), no trajectory starting in
\mathcal{S}_{c}^{C}
can reach the set
(0,1]\times\{0\}
, therefore
\mathcal{S}_{c}^{C}\cap\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}=\varnothing
and this implies that (
3.167
) is true.
As a result, the proof of (
3.166
) is established and the proof is completed for
\rho=1
.

(ii)
Let
\rho<1
. For the sake of simplicity, we suppose
that
\frac{\rho c(c+1)}{1+\rho c}\geq 1
.
Let
\mathcal{Y}
be the set in the right-hand-side of (
3.16
), and

\mathcal{F}_{0}:=\big\{(u,v)\in[0,1]\times[0,1]\;{\mbox{ s.t. }}\;v<\gamma_{0}(u)\,\text{ if }\,u\in[0,1]\big\}.

(3.171)

Notice that

\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{F}_{0}\cup\mathcal{P},

(3.172)

being
\mathcal{P}
the set defined in (
3.145
).

Moreover,

\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}},

(3.173)

thanks to
Proposition
3.22
.

We also claim that

\mathcal{F}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{K}},

(3.174)

where
\mathcal{K}
is the set of constant functions.
Indeed, if
(u,v)\in\mathcal{F}_{0}
, we have that
v<\gamma_{0}(u)

and consequently
v<\gamma_{a}(u)
, as long as
a
is small enough,
due to Lemma
3.21
.

From this and Proposition
3.16
, we deduce that
(u,v)

belongs to
{\mathcal{E}}(a)
, as long as
a
is small enough,
and this proves (
3.174
).

From (
3.174
) and the fact that
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
,
we obtain that

\mathcal{F}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}.

(3.175)

Then, as a consequence of (
3.172
),
(
3.173
) and (
3.175
),
we get that
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
.

Hence, we are left with proving that

\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq\mathcal{Y}.

(3.176)

For this, we show that

on
\partial\mathcal{Y}\cap\big((0,1)\times(0,1)\big)
the outward normal derivative is nonnegative.

(3.177)

To prove this,
we calculate the outward normal derivative on the part of
\partial\mathcal{Y}
lying on the graph of
v=\gamma_{0}(u)
, that is

\dot{v}-\frac{u^{{\rho}-1}\dot{u}}{c(u^{0}_{s})^{\rho-1}}={\rho}v(1-u-v)-au-\frac{u^{{\rho}}(1-u-v-ac)}{c(u^{0}_{s})^{\rho-1}}.

By substituting
v=\gamma_{0}(u)=\frac{u^{\rho}}{\rho c(u_{s}^{0})^{\rho-1}}
we get

\displaystyle\dot{v}-\frac{u^{{\rho}-1}\dot{u}}{c(u^{0}_{s})^{\rho-1}}=\frac{u^{\rho}}{c(u_{s}^{0})^{\rho-1}}(1-u-v)-au-\frac{u^{{\rho}}(1-u-v-ac)}{c(u^{0}_{s})^{\rho-1}}

\displaystyle\qquad=-au+\frac{acu^{{\rho}}}{c(u^{0}_{s})^{\rho-1}}=au^{\rho}\left(-u^{1-{\rho}}+\frac{1}{(u^{0}_{s})^{\rho-1}}\right).

As a result, since
{\rho}<1
, we have

\dot{v}-\frac{u^{{\rho}-1}\dot{u}}{c(u^{0}_{s})^{\rho-1}}\geq 0\quad\text{for}\ u\leq u_{s}^{0}.

(3.178)

On the part of
\partial\mathcal{Y}
contained on the line
v=\frac{u}{c}+\frac{1-{\rho}}{1+{\rho}c}
, the outward normal derivative is

\begin{split}&\dot{v}-\frac{\dot{u}}{c}={\rho}v(1-u-v)-au-\frac{u(1-ac-u-v)}{c}=\left({\rho}v-\frac{u}{c}\right)(1-u-v)\\
&\qquad\qquad=\left(\frac{\rho u}{c}+\frac{\rho(1-{\rho})}{1+{\rho}c}-\frac{u}{c}\right)\left(1-u-\frac{u}{c}-\frac{1-{\rho}}{1+{\rho}c}\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad=\left(\frac{(\rho-1)u}{c}+\frac{\rho(1-{\rho})}{1+{\rho}c}\right)\left(-\frac{u(c+1)}{c}+\frac{\rho(1+c)}{1+{\rho}c}\right).\end{split}

(3.179)

We also observe that, when
u>u_{s}^{0}=\frac{\rho c}{1+\rho c}
, the condition
\rho<1
gives that

\displaystyle\frac{(\rho-1)u}{c}+\frac{\rho(1-{\rho})}{1+{\rho}c}<\frac{\rho(\rho-1)}{1+\rho c}+\frac{\rho(1-{\rho})}{1+{\rho}c}=0

and

\displaystyle-\frac{u(c+1)}{c}+\frac{\rho(1+c)}{1+{\rho}c}<-\frac{\rho(c+1)}{1+\rho c}+\frac{\rho(1+c)}{1+{\rho}c}=0.

Therefore, when
u>u_{s}^{0}
, we deduce from (
3.179
) that

\dot{v}-\frac{\dot{u}}{c}>0.

Combining this and (
3.178
), we obtain (
3.177
), as desired.

Now, by (
3.177
), we have that, for any value of
a
, no trajectory starting in
\big([0,1]\times[0,1]\big)\setminus\mathcal{Y}
can enter in
\mathcal{Y}
,
and in particular no trajectory starting
in
\big([0,1]\times[0,1]\big)\setminus\mathcal{Y}

can hit
\{v=0\}
, which ends the proof of (
3.176
).

(iii)
Let
\rho>1
. For the sake of simplicity, we suppose
that
\frac{c}{(c+1)^{\rho}}\geq 1
.
Let
\mathcal{X}
be the right-hand-side of (
3.17
).
We observe that

\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{S}_{c}\cup\mathcal{Q},

(3.180)

where
\mathcal{S}_{c}
was defined in (
3.114
) and
\mathcal{Q}
in (
3.147
).
Thanks to Proposition
3.20
,
one has that
\mathcal{S}_{c}\subseteq\underset{a>a^{\prime}}{\bigcup}\mathcal{E}(a)
, for every
a^{\prime}>0
, and therefore
\mathcal{S}_{c}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
.
Moreover, by the second claim in
Proposition
3.22
, one also has that
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
. Hence,

\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}.

(3.181)

Accordingly, to prove equality in (
3.181
) and thus complete
the proof of (
3.17
), we need to show that
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
.
First, we prove that

(0,1]\times\{0\}\subseteq\mathcal{X}.

(3.182)

Indeed, for
u>0
we have
v=\frac{u}{c}>0
, therefore
(u,0)\in\mathcal{X}
for
u\in(0,u_{\infty}]
. Then,
\zeta(u)
is increasing in
u
since it is a positive power function, therefore
v=\zeta(u)>0
for
u\in(u_{\infty},1]
, hence
(u,0)\in\mathcal{X}
for
u\in(u_{\infty},1]
. These observations prove (
3.182
).

We now prove that the component of the velocity field in the outward normal direction with respect to
\mathcal{X}
is nonnegative on

\partial\mathcal{X}\cap\partial(\mathcal{X}^{C})=\\
\left\{(u,v)\in(0,u_{\infty}]\times(0,1)\ :\ v=\frac{u}{c}\right\}\cup\left\{(u,v)\in(u_{\infty},1)\times(0,1)\>\ v=\zeta(u)\right\}.

To this end. we observe that on the line
v=\frac{u}{c}
, the outward normal derivative is

\dot{v}-\frac{1}{c}\dot{u}=\rho v(1-u-v)-au-\frac{u}{c}(1-ac-u-v)=(\rho v-\frac{u}{c})(1-u-v).

(3.183)

The first term is positive because for
\rho>1
we have

\rho v>v=\frac{u}{c}.

Moreover, for
u\leq u_{\infty}
we have that

1-u-v\geq 1-u_{\infty}-\frac{u_{\infty}}{c}=0,

thanks to (
3.18
).
Thus, the left hand side of (
3.183
) is nonnegative,
which proves that the component of the velocity field in the outward normal direction is nonnegative
on
\partial\mathcal{X}\cap\left\{v=\frac{u}{c}\right\}
.

On the part of
\partial\mathcal{X}
lying in the graph of
v=\zeta(u)
, the component of the velocity field in the outward normal direction is
given by

\dot{v}-\frac{\rho u^{{\rho}-1}\dot{u}}{\rho c(u_{\infty})^{\rho-1}}={\rho}v(1-u-v)-au-\frac{{\rho}u^{{\rho}}}{\rho c(u_{\infty})^{\rho-1}}(1-u-v-ac).

(3.184)

Now we substitute
v=\zeta(u)=\frac{u^{{\rho}}}{\rho c(u_{\infty})^{\rho-1}}
in (
3.184
) and we get

\displaystyle\dot{v}-\frac{u^{{\rho}-1}\dot{u}}{c(u_{\infty})^{\rho-1}}=au\left(-1+\frac{u^{\rho-1}}{(u_{\infty})^{\rho-1}}\right)

which leads to

\dot{v}-\frac{\rho u^{{\rho}-1}\dot{u}}{\rho c(u_{\infty})^{\rho-1}}>0\quad\text{if}\ u>u_{\infty},

as desired.

As a consequence of these considerations, we find that no trajectory starting in
\mathcal{X}^{C}
can enter in
\mathcal{X}
and therefore hit
\{v=0\}
, by (
3.182
). Hence, we conclude that
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
, which, together with (
3.181
), establishes (
3.17
).
∎

3.4.3
Proof of Theorem
3.4

In order to prove Theorem
3.4
,
we will establish a geometrical lemma in order to
understand the reciprocal position of the function
\gamma
,
as given by Propositions
3.8
and
3.14
,
and the straight line where the saddle equilibria lie. To emphasize the dependence of
\gamma

on the parameter
a
we will often use the notation
\gamma=\gamma_{a}
.
Moreover, we recall the notation of the saddle points
(u_{s},v_{s})

defined in (
3.11
) and of the points
(u_{\mathcal{M}},v_{\mathcal{M}})
given by
Propositions
3.8
and
3.14
, with the convention
that

(u_{s},v_{s})=(0,0)
if
ac\geq 1
,

(3.185)

and we state the following
result:

Lemma 3.24
.

If
\rho<1
, then

\frac{u}{{\rho}c}\leq\gamma_{a}(u)\quad\text{ for }u\in[0,u_{s}]

(3.186)

and

\gamma_{a}(u)\leq\frac{u}{{\rho}c}\quad\text{ for }u\in[u_{s},u_{\mathcal{M}}].

(3.187)

If instead
{\rho}>1
, then

\gamma_{a}(u)\leq\frac{u}{{\rho}c}\quad\text{ for }u\in[0,u_{s}]

(3.188)

and

\frac{u}{{\rho}c}\leq\gamma_{a}(u)\quad\text{ for }u\in[u_{s},u_{\mathcal{M}}].

(3.189)

Moreover equality holds in (
3.186
) and (
3.188
)
if and only if either
u=u_{s}
or
u=0
. Also, strict inequality
holds in (
3.187
)
and (
3.189
) for
u\in(u_{s},u_{\mathcal{M}})
.

Proof.

We focus here on the proof of (
3.187
), since
the other inequalities are proven in a similar way. Moreover,
we deal with the case
ac<1
, being the case
ac\geq 1
analogous with
obvious modifications.

We suppose by contradiction that (
3.187
) does not hold true.
Namely, we assume that there exists
\tilde{u}\in(u_{s},u_{\mathcal{M}}]
such that

\gamma_{a}(\tilde{u})>\frac{\tilde{u}}{{\rho}c}.

Since
\gamma_{a}
is continuous thanks to Propositions
3.8
,
we have that

\gamma_{a}(u)>\frac{u}{{\rho}c}\quad\mbox{in a neighborhood of\penalty\ $\tilde{u}$. }

Hence, we consider the largest open
interval
(u_{1},u_{2})\subset(u_{s},u_{\mathcal{M}}]
containing
\tilde{u}
and such that

\gamma_{a}(u)>\frac{u}{{\rho}c}\quad{\mbox{ for all }}u\in(u_{1},u_{2}).

(3.190)

Moreover, in light of (
3.11
), we see that

\gamma_{a}(u_{s})=v_{s}=\frac{1-ac}{1+\rho c}=\frac{u_{s}}{\rho c}.

(3.191)

Hence, by the continuity of
\gamma_{a}
, we have that
\gamma_{a}(u_{1})=\frac{u_{1}}{\rho c}

and

either
\gamma_{a}(u_{2})=\displaystyle\frac{u_{2}}{\rho c}
or
u_{2}=u_{\mathcal{M}}
.

(3.192)

Now, we consider the set

\mathcal{T}:=\left\{(u,v)\in[u_{1},u_{2}]\times[0,1]\;{\mbox{ s.t. }}\;\frac{u}{{\rho}c}<v<\gamma_{a}(u)\right\},

that is non empty, thanks to (
3.190
).
We claim that

for all
(u(0),v(0))\in\mathcal{T}
, the
\omega
-limit
of its trajectory is
(u_{s},v_{s})
.

(3.193)

To prove this,
we analyze the normal derivative on

\begin{split}&\partial\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{1}\cup\mathcal{T}_{2}\cup\mathcal{T}_{3},\\
{\mbox{where }}\quad&\mathcal{T}_{1}:=\big\{(u,\gamma_{a}(u))\;{\mbox{ with }}u\in(u_{1},u_{2})\big\},\\
&\mathcal{T}_{2}:=\left\{\left(u,\frac{u}{{\rho}c}\right)\;{\mbox{ with }}u\in(u_{1},u_{2})\right\}\\
{\mbox{and }}\quad&\mathcal{T}_{3}:=\left\{(u_{2},v)\;{\mbox{ with }}v\in\left(\frac{u_{2}}{{\rho}c},\min\{\gamma_{a}(u_{2}),1\}\right)\right\},\end{split}

with the convention that
\partial\mathcal{T}\penalty\
does contain
\mathcal{T}_{3}

only if the second possibility in (
3.192
) occurs.

We notice that the set
\mathcal{T}_{1}
is an orbit for the system, and
thus the component of the velocity in the normal direction is null.
On
\mathcal{T}_{2}
, we have that the sign of
the component of the velocity in the inward normal direction is given by

\begin{split}&(\dot{u},\dot{v})\cdot\left(-\frac{1}{\rho c},1\right)=\dot{v}-\frac{1}{{\rho}c}\dot{u}={\rho}v(1-u-v)-au-\frac{u}{{\rho}c}(1-u-v)+\frac{au}{\rho}\\
&\qquad\qquad=\frac{u}{c}\left(1-u-\frac{u}{{\rho}c}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{{\rho}}\right)-au\left(1-\frac{1}{\rho}\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad=\frac{u}{c}\left(1-\frac{1}{\rho}\right)\left(1-u-\frac{u}{{\rho}c}-ac\right).\end{split}

(3.194)

Notice that for
u\geq u_{s}
we have that

1-u-v-ac\leq 0,

(3.195)

thus the sign of last term in (
3.194
) depends only on
the quantity
1-\frac{1}{\rho}
.
Consequently, since
{\rho}<1
the sign of
the component of the velocity in the inward normal direction is positive.

Furthermore, in the case in which the second possibility in (
3.192
) occurs,
we also check the sign of the component of the velocity in the inward normal direction
along
\mathcal{T}_{3}
. In this case, if
\gamma_{a}(u_{2})<1
then
u_{2}=1
,
and therefore we find that

(\dot{u},\dot{v})\cdot\left(-1,0\right)=-\dot{u}=-u(1-u-v)+acu=v+ac,

which is positive. If instead
\gamma_{a}(u_{2})=1

(\dot{u},\dot{v})\cdot\left(-1,0\right)=-\dot{u}=-u(1-u-v)+acu=-u(1-ac-u-v),

which is positive, thanks to (
3.195
).

We also point out that there are no cycle in
\mathcal{T}
, since
\dot{u}

has a sign. These considerations and the
Poincaré-Bendixson Theorem (see e.g.
[
113
]
)
give that the
\omega
-limit set of
(u(0),v(0))

can be either an equilibrium or a union of (finitely many)
equilibria and non-closed orbits connecting these equilibria.
Since
(0,0)
and
(0,1)
do not belong to the closure of
\mathcal{T}
,
in this case the only possibility is that the
\omega
-limit is the equilibrium
(u_{s},v_{s})
.
Consequently, we have that
u_{1}=u_{s}
, and that (
3.193
)
is satisfied.

Accordingly, in light of (
3.193
), we have that the
set
\mathcal{T}

is contained in the stable manifold of
(u_{s},v_{s})
, which is in contradiction
with the definition of
\mathcal{T}
.
Hence, (
3.187
) is established, as desired.

Now we show that strict inequality holds true in (
3.187
)
if
u\in(u_{s},u_{\mathcal{M}})
. To this end, we suppose by contradiction that
there exists
\bar{u}\in(u_{s},u_{\mathcal{M}})
such that

\gamma_{a}(\bar{u})=\frac{\bar{u}}{{\rho}c}.

(3.196)

Now, since (
3.187
) holds true, we have that
the line
v-\frac{u}{{\rho}c}=0
is tangent to the curve
v=\gamma_{a}(u)
at
(\bar{u},\gamma_{a}(\bar{u}))
,
and therefore at this point the components of the velocity
along the normal directions to the curve and to the line coincide.
On the other hand,
the normal derivative at a point on
the line has a sign, as computed in (
3.194
), while the normal derivative
to
v=\gamma_{a}(u)
is
0
because the curve is an orbit.

This, together with (
3.191
), proves that equality
in (
3.187
) holds true if
u=u_{s}
, but strict inequality holds true
for all
u\in(u_{s},u_{\mathcal{M}})
,
and thus
the proof of Lemma
3.24
is complete.
∎

For each
a>0
, we define
(u_{d}^{a},v_{d}^{a})\in[0,1]\times[0,1]
as the unique intersection of the graph of
\gamma_{a}
with the line
\{v=1-u\}
, that is the solution of the system

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}v_{d}^{a}=\gamma_{a}(u_{d}^{a}),\\
v_{d}^{a}=1-u_{d}^{a}.\end{array}\right.

(3.197)

We recall that the above intersection is
unique since the function
\gamma_{a}

is increasing. Also, by construction,

u_{d}^{a}\leq u_{\mathcal{M}}.

(3.198)

Now, recalling (
3.11
) and making explicit the dependence on
a
by writing
u_{s}^{a}

(with the convention in (
3.185
)), we give the following result:

Lemma 3.25
.

We have that:

1.

For
\rho<1
, for all
a^{*}>0
it holds that

\gamma_{a}(u)\leq\gamma_{a^{*}}(u)\quad\text{for all}\ a>a^{*}\ \text{and for all}\ u\in[u_{s}^{a^{*}},u_{d}^{a^{*}}].

(3.199)

2.

For
\rho>1
, for all
a^{*}>0
it holds that

\gamma_{a}(u)\leq\gamma_{a^{*}}(u)\quad\text{for all}\ a<a^{*}\ \text{and for all}\ u\in[u_{s}^{a^{*}},u_{d}^{a^{*}}].

(3.200)

Proof.

We claim that

u_{s}^{a^{*}}<u_{d}^{a^{*}}.

(3.201)

Indeed, when
a^{*}c\geq 1
, we have that
u_{s}^{a^{*}}=0<u_{d}^{a^{*}}

and thus (
3.201
) holds true. If instead
a^{*}c<1
, by (
3.11
) and (
3.197
) we have that

\gamma_{a^{*}}(u_{s}^{a^{*}})+u_{s}^{a^{*}}=1-a^{*}c<1=\gamma_{a^{*}}(u_{d}^{a^{*}})+u_{d}^{a^{*}}.

(3.202)

Also, since
\gamma_{a^{*}}
is increasing,
we have that the map
r\mapsto\gamma_{a^{*}}(r)+r

is strictly increasing. Consequently, we deduce from (
3.202
) that (
3.201
)
holds true in this case as well.

Now we suppose that
\rho<1
and we prove (
3.199
).
For this, we claim that, for every
a^{*}>0
and every
a>a^{*}
,

\gamma_{a}(u_{s}^{a^{*}})\leq\gamma_{a^{*}}(u_{s}^{a^{*}})\quad{\mbox{ with strict inequality when }}a^{*}\in\left(0,\frac{1}{c}\right).

(3.203)

To check this, we distinguish two cases.
If
a^{*}\in\left(0,\frac{1}{c}\right)
, then for all
a>a^{*}

u_{s}^{a}=\max\left\{0,\rho c\frac{1-ac}{1+\rho c}\right\}<\rho c\frac{1-a^{*}c}{1+\rho c}=u_{s}^{a^{*}}.

(3.204)

By (
3.204
) and formula (
3.187
) in Lemma
3.24
, we have that

\gamma_{a}(u_{s}^{a^{*}})<\frac{u_{s}^{a^{*}}}{\rho c}=\gamma_{a^{*}}(u_{s}^{a^{*}})\quad\text{for all}\ a>a^{*}.

(3.205)

If instead
a^{*}\geq\frac{1}{c}
, then
u_{s}^{a^{*}}=0
and for all
a>a^{*}
we have
u_{s}^{a}=0
. As a consequence,

\gamma_{{a^{*}}}(u_{s}^{a^{*}})=\gamma_{{a}}(u_{s}^{a^{*}})\quad\text{for all}\ a>a^{*}.

(3.206)

The claim in (
3.203
) thus follows from (
3.205
) and (
3.206
).

Furthermore, by Propositions
3.8
and
3.14
,

\gamma_{a}^{\prime}(0)=\frac{a}{\rho+ac-1}<\frac{{a^{*}}}{\rho+{a^{*}}c-1}=\gamma_{a^{*}}^{\prime}(0)\quad\text{for all}\ a>a^{*}\geq\frac{1}{c}.

(3.207)

Moreover, for all
a\geq a^{*}
and
u>u_{s}^{a^{*}}
it holds that, when
v=\gamma_{a^{*}}(u)
,

-\big(acu-u(1-u-v)\big)=u(1-u-\gamma_{a^{*}}(u)-ac)<u(1-u_{s}^{a^{*}}-v_{s}^{a^{*}}-ac)\leq 0.

(3.208)

Now, we establish that

u(\rho cv-u)(1-u-v)(a-a^{*})<0\ \ \text{for all}\ a>a^{*},\ u\in(u_{s}^{a^{*}},u_{d}^{a^{*}}),\ v=\gamma_{a^{*}}(u).

(3.209)

Indeed,
for the values of
a
,
u
and
v
as in (
3.209
) we have
that
v\leq\gamma_{a^{*}}(u_{d}^{a^{*}})
and hence

(1-u-v)>(1-u_{d}^{a^{*}}-\gamma_{a^{*}}(u_{d}^{a^{*}}))=0.

(3.210)

Moreover, by formula (
3.187
) in Lemma
3.24
,
for
u\in(u_{s}^{a^{*}},u_{d}^{a^{*}})
and
v=\gamma_{a^{*}}(u)
and we have that

\rho cv-u=\rho c\gamma_{a^{*}}(u)-u<0.

From this and (
3.210
), we see that (
3.209
) plainly follows, as desired.

As a consequence of (
3.208
) and (
3.209
), one deduces that,
for all
a>a^{*}
,
u\in(u_{s}^{a^{*}},u_{d}^{a^{*}})
and
v=\gamma_{a^{*}}(u)
,

\begin{split}&\frac{au-\rho v(1-u-v)}{acu-u(1-u-v)}-\frac{a^{*}u-\rho v(1-u-v)}{a^{*}cu-u(1-u-v)}\\
=\,&\frac{(a-a^{*})c\rho uv(1-u-v)-(a-a^{*})u^{2}(1-u-v)}{\big(acu-u(1-u-v)\big)\big(a^{*}cu-u(1-u-v)\big)}\\
=\,&\frac{(a-a^{*})(1-u-v)u(c\rho v-u)}{\big(acu-u(1-u-v)\big)\big(a^{*}cu-u(1-u-v)\big)}\\
\leq\,&0.\end{split}

(3.211)

Now, we define

{\mathcal{Z}}(u):=\gamma_{a}(u)-\gamma_{a^{*}}(u)

(3.212)

and we claim that

if
u_{o}\in(u_{s}^{a^{*}},u_{d}^{a^{*}})
is such that
{\mathcal{Z}}(u_{o})=0
,
then
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}(u_{o})<0
.

(3.213)

Indeed,
since
\gamma_{a}
is a trajectory for (
3.1
),
if
(u_{a}(t),v_{a}(t))
is a solution of (
3.1
), we have that
v_{a}(t)=\gamma_{a}(u_{a}(t))
, whence

\begin{split}&\rho v_{a}(t)(1-u_{a}(t)-v_{a}(t))-au_{a}(t)=\dot{v}_{a}(t)=\gamma_{a}^{\prime}(u_{a}(t))\,\dot{u}_{a}(t)\\
&\qquad=\gamma_{a}^{\prime}(u_{a}(t))\big(u_{a}(t)(1-u_{a}(t)-v_{a}(t))-acu_{a}(t)\big).\end{split}

(3.214)

Then, we let
v_{o}:=\gamma_{a}(u_{o})

and we notice that
v_{o}
coincides also with
\gamma_{a^{*}}(u_{o})
.
Hence, we
take trajectories of the system with parameter
a

and
a^{*}
starting at
(u_{o},v_{o})
,
and by (
3.211
) we obtain that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\frac{au_{o}-\rho v(1-u_{o}-v_{o})}{acu_{o}-u_{o}(1-u_{o}-v_{o})}-\frac{a^{*}u_{o}-\rho v(1-u_{o}-v_{o})}{a^{*}cu_{o}-u_{o}(1-u_{o}-v_{o})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{au_{a}(0)-\rho v(1-u_{a}(0)-v_{a}(0))}{acu_{a}(0)-u(1-u_{a}(0)-v_{a}(0))}-\frac{a^{*}u_{a^{*}}(0)-\rho v(1-u_{a^{*}}(0)-v_{a}(0))}{a^{*}cu_{a^{*}}(0)-u(1-u_{a^{*}}(0)-v_{a^{*}}(0))}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}_{a}(u_{a}(0))-\gamma^{\prime}_{a^{*}}(u_{a^{*}}(0))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}_{a}(u_{o})-\gamma^{\prime}_{a^{*}}(u_{o}),

which establishes (
3.213
).

Now we claim that

\begin{split}&{\mbox{there exists\penalty\ $\underline{u}\in[u_{s}^{a^{*}},u_{d}^{a^{*}}]$
such that\penalty\ ${\mathcal{Z}}(\underline{u})<0$}}\\
&{\mbox{and\penalty\ ${\mathcal{Z}}(u)\leq 0$ for every\penalty\ $u\in[u_{s}^{a^{*}},\underline{u}]$.}}\end{split}

(3.215)

Indeed, if
a^{*}\in\left(0,\frac{1}{c}\right)
,
we deduce from (
3.203
)
that
{\mathcal{Z}}(u_{s}^{a^{*}})<0

and therefore (
3.215
) holds true with
\underline{u}:=u_{s}^{a^{*}}
. If instead
a^{*}\geq\frac{1}{c}
,
we have that
u_{s}^{a}=u_{s}^{a^{*}}=0

and we deduce from (
3.203
)
and (
3.207
) that
{\mathcal{Z}}(u_{s}^{a^{*}})=0

and
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}(u_{s}^{a^{*}})<0
, from which (
3.215
)
follows by choosing
\underline{u}:=u_{s}^{a^{*}}+\epsilon

with
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small.

Now we claim that

{\mathcal{Z}}(u)\leq 0\qquad{\mbox{for every }}u\in[u_{s}^{a^{*}},u_{d}^{a^{*}}].

(3.216)

To prove this,
in light of (
3.215
), it suffices to check that
{\mathcal{Z}}(u)\leq 0

for every
u\in(\underline{u},u_{d}^{a^{*}}]
.
Suppose not. Then there exists
u^{\sharp}\in(\underline{u},u_{d}^{a^{*}}]

such that
{\mathcal{Z}}(u)<0
for all
[\underline{u},u^{\sharp})

and
{\mathcal{Z}}(u^{\sharp})=0
. This gives that
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}(u^{\sharp})\geq 0
.
But this inequality is in contradiction with (
3.213
)
and therefore the proof of (
3.216
)
is complete.

The desired claim in (
3.199
) follows easily
from (
3.216
), hence we focus now
on the proof of (
3.200
).

To this end, we take
\rho>1
and we
claim that, for every
a^{*}>0
and every
a\in(0,a^{*})
,

\gamma_{a}(u_{s}^{a^{*}})\leq\gamma_{a^{*}}(u_{s}^{a^{*}})\quad{\mbox{ with strict inequality when }}a^{*}\in\left(0,\frac{1}{c}\right).

(3.217)

To prove this, we first notice that,
if
a<a^{*}<\frac{1}{c}
, then

u_{s}^{a^{*}}=\rho c\frac{1-a^{*}c}{1+\rho c}<\rho c\frac{1-ac}{1+\rho c}=u_{s}^{a}.

Hence by (
3.188
) in
Lemma
3.24
we have

\gamma_{a}(u_{s}^{a^{*}})<\frac{u_{s}^{a^{*}}}{\rho c}=\gamma_{a^{*}}(u_{s}^{a^{*}})\quad\text{for}\ a<a^{*}<\frac{1}{c},

and this establishes (
3.217
)
when
a^{*}\in\left(0,\frac{1}{c}\right)
.
Thus, we now focus on the case
a^{*}\geq\frac{1}{c}
.
In this situation, we have that
u_{s}^{a^{*}}=0

and accordingly
\gamma_{a}(u_{s}^{a^{*}})=\gamma_{a}(0)=\gamma_{a^{*}}(0)=\gamma_{a^{*}}(u_{s}^{a^{*}})
, that completes the proof
of (
3.217
).

In addition, by Propositions
3.8
and
3.14
we have that

\gamma_{a}^{\prime}(0)=\frac{a}{\rho-1+ac}\leq\frac{{a^{*}}}{\rho-1+{a^{*}}c}=\gamma_{a^{*}}^{\prime}(0)\quad\text{for}\ a\in\left[\frac{1}{c},{a^{*}}\right].

(3.218)

Moreover, for
u>u_{s}^{a}
, if
v=\gamma_{a}(u)
we have that
v>\gamma_{a}(u_{s}^{a})=v_{s}^{a}
, thanks to the monotonicity of
\gamma_{a}
,
and, as a result,

u(1-u-v-ac)<u(1-u_{s}^{a}-v_{s}^{a}-ac)=0.

(3.219)

Now we claim that, for all
a<{a^{*}}
,

u\in(u_{s}^{a^{*}},u_{d}^{{a^{*}}})
and
v=\gamma_{{a^{*}}}(u)
,
we have

u(1-u-v)({a^{*}}-a)(u-\rho cv)<0.

(3.220)

Indeed,
by the monotonicity of
\gamma_{{a^{*}}}
,
in this situation we have that
v\leq\gamma_{{a^{*}}}(u^{a^{*}}_{d})
,
and therefore,
by (
3.197
),

1-u-v>1-u_{d}^{a^{*}}-\gamma_{{a^{*}}}(u_{d}^{a^{*}})=1-u_{d}^{a^{*}}-1+u_{d}^{a^{*}}=0.

(3.221)

Moreover, by (
3.189
)
in Lemma (
3.24
),
we have that
\gamma_{a^{*}}(u)>\frac{u}{\rho c}
, and
hence
u-\rho cv>0
. Combining this inequality with (
3.221
),
we obtain (
3.220
), as desired.

Now, by (
3.219
),
for all
a<{a^{*}}
,

u\in(u_{s}^{a},u_{d}^{{a^{*}}})

and
v=\gamma_{{a^{*}}}(u)
,

0<-u(1-u-v-ac)=acu-u(1-u-v)<{a^{*}}cu-u(1-u-v)

and then, by (
3.220
),

\begin{split}&\frac{au-\rho v(1-u-v)}{acu-u(1-u-v)}-\frac{{a^{*}}u-\rho v(1-u-v)}{{a^{*}}cu-u(1-u-v)}\\
=\,&\frac{u(1-u-v)({a^{*}}-a)(u-\rho cv)}{\big(acu-u(1-u-v)\big)\big({a^{*}}cu-u(1-u-v)\big)}\\
<\,&0.\end{split}

(3.222)

Now we recall the definition of
{\mathcal{Z}}

in (
3.212
)
and we claim that

if
u_{o}\in(u_{s}^{a^{*}},u_{d}^{a^{*}})
is such that
{\mathcal{Z}}(u_{o})=0
,
then
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}(u_{o})<0
.

(3.223)

To prove this, we let
v_{o}:=\gamma_{a}(u_{o})
,
we notice that
v_{o}=\gamma_{a^{*}}(u_{o})
, we
recall (
3.214
)
and apply it to a trajectory starting at
(u_{o},v_{o})
,
thus finding that

\displaystyle\rho v_{o}(1-u_{o}-v_{a}(t))-au_{o}=\gamma_{a}^{\prime}(u_{o})\big(u_{o}(1-u_{o}-v_{o})-acu_{o}\big).

This and (
3.222
) yield that

\displaystyle 0>\frac{au-\rho v(1-u-v)}{acu-u(1-u-v)}-\frac{{a^{*}}u-\rho v(1-u-v)}{{a^{*}}cu-u(1-u-v)}=\gamma_{a}^{\prime}(u_{o})-\gamma_{a^{*}}^{\prime}(u_{o})={\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}(u_{o}),

which proves the desired claim in (
3.223
).

We now point out that

\begin{split}&{\mbox{there exists\penalty\ $\underline{u}\in[u_{s}^{a^{*}},u_{d}^{a^{*}}]$
such that\penalty\ ${\mathcal{Z}}(\underline{u})<0$}}\\
&{\mbox{and\penalty\ ${\mathcal{Z}}(u)\leq 0$ for every\penalty\ $u\in[u_{s}^{a^{*}},\underline{u}]$.}}\end{split}

(3.224)

Indeed, if
a^{*}\in\left(0,\frac{1}{c}\right)
,
this claim follows directly from (
3.203
)
by choosing
\underline{u}:=u_{s}^{a^{*}}
, while if
a^{*}\geq\frac{1}{c}
,
the claim follows from (
3.203
)
and (
3.213
)
by choosing
\underline{u}:=u_{s}^{a^{*}}+\epsilon

with
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small.

Now we claim that

{\mathcal{Z}}(u)\leq 0\qquad{\mbox{for every }}u\in[u_{s}^{a^{*}},u_{d}^{a^{*}}].

(3.225)

Indeed, by (
3.224
),
we know that the claim is true for all
u\in[u_{s}^{a^{*}},\underline{u}]
.
Then, the claim for
u\in(\underline{u},u_{d}^{a^{*}}]

can be proved by contradiction,
supposing that there exists
u^{\sharp}\in(\underline{u},u_{d}^{a^{*}}]

such that
{\mathcal{Z}}(u)<0
for all
[\underline{u},u^{\sharp})

and
{\mathcal{Z}}(u^{\sharp})=0
. This gives that
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}(u^{\sharp})\geq 0
, which is in contradiction with (
3.213
).

Having completed the proof of (
3.225
),
one can use it to obtain the desired claim in (
3.200
).
∎

Now we perform the proof
of Theorem
3.4
, analyzing separately the cases
\rho=1
,
\rho<1

and
\rho>1
.

Proof of Theorem
3.4
, case
\rho=1
.

We notice that

\mathcal{V_{\mathcal{K}}}\subseteq\mathcal{V_{\mathcal{A}}},

(3.226)

since
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{A}
.

Also, from Theorem
3.3
, part (i), we get that
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{S}_{c}
, where
\mathcal{S}_{c}
was defined in (
3.114
).
On the other hand, by Lemma
3.23
, we know that for
\rho=1
and for all
a>0
we have
{\mathcal{E}(a)}=\mathcal{S}_{c}
.
But since every constant
a
belongs to the set
\mathcal{K}
, we have
\mathcal{E}(a)\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{K}}
.
This shows that
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{E}(a)}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{K}}
, and together with (
3.226
) concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.4
, case
\rho<1
.

We notice that

\mathcal{V_{\mathcal{K}}}\subseteq\mathcal{V_{\mathcal{A}}},

(3.227)

since
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{A}
.
To prove that the inclusion is strict, we aim to find a point
(\bar{u},\bar{v})\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{K}}
.
Namely, we have to prove that there exists
(\bar{u},\bar{v})\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
such that,
for all constant strategies
a>0
, we have that
(\bar{u},\bar{v})\notin\mathcal{E}(a)
,
that is, by the characterization in Proposition
3.16
,
it must hold true that
\bar{v}\geq\gamma_{a}(\bar{u})
and
\bar{u}\leq u_{\mathcal{M}}^{a}
.

To do this, we define

f(u):=\frac{u}{c}+\frac{1-\rho}{1+\rho c}\quad{\mbox{ and }}\quad m:=\min\left\{\frac{\rho c(c+1)}{1+\rho c},1\right\}.

(3.228)

By inspection, one can see that
(u,f(u))\in[0,1]\times[0,1]
if and only
if
u\in[0,m]
.
We point out that, by (ii) of Theorem
3.3
,
for
\rho<1
and
u\in[u_{s}^{0},m]
,
a point
({u},{v})
belongs to
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}

if and only if
{v}<f({u})
. Here
u_{s}^{0}
is defined in (
3.14
). We underline that the interval
[u_{s}^{0},m]
is non empty since

u_{s}^{0}=\frac{\rho c}{1+\rho c}<\min\left\{\frac{\rho c(c+1)}{1+\rho c},1\right\}=m.

(3.229)

Now we point out that

m\leq u_{\mathcal{M}}^{a}.

(3.230)

Indeed, by (
3.228
) we already know that
m\leq 1
, thus if
u_{\mathcal{M}}^{a}=1
the inequality in (
3.230
) is true. On the other hand, when
u_{\mathcal{M}}^{a}<1
we have that
(u_{\mathcal{M}}^{a},1)\times(0,1)\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}(a)
.
This and (
3.227
) give that
(u_{\mathcal{M}}^{a},1)\times(0,1)\subseteq\mathcal{V_{\mathcal{K}}}\subseteq\mathcal{V_{\mathcal{A}}}
. Hence, in view of (
3.16
), we deduce that
\frac{\rho c(c+1)}{1+\rho c}\leq u_{\mathcal{M}}^{a}
. In particular, we find that
m\leq u_{\mathcal{M}}^{a}
,
and therefore (
3.230
) is true also in this case.

With this notation, we claim the existence of a
value
\bar{v}\in(0,1]

such that for all
a>0
we have

\gamma_{a}(m)\leq\bar{v}<f(m)
.
That is, we prove now that there
exists
\theta>0
such that

\gamma_{a}(m)+\theta<f(m)\quad{\mbox{ for all }}a>0.

(3.231)

The strategy is to study two cases separately, namely we prove (
3.231
)
for sufficiently small values of
a
and then for the other
values of
a
.

To prove (
3.231
) for small values of
a
, we start by
looking at
the limit function
\gamma_{0}
defined in (
3.108
).
One observes that

\gamma_{0}(u_{s}^{0})=v_{s}^{0}=\frac{1}{1+\rho c}=\frac{\rho c}{c(1+\rho c)}+\frac{1-\rho}{1+\rho c}=f(u_{s}^{0}).

(3.232)

Moreover, for all
u\in(u_{s}^{0},m]
,
we have that

\gamma_{0}^{\prime}(u)=\frac{v_{s}^{0}}{(u_{s}^{0})^{\rho}}\,\rho u^{\rho-1}<\frac{v_{s}^{0}}{(u_{s}^{0})^{\rho}}\,\rho(u_{s}^{0})^{\rho-1}=\frac{\rho v_{s}^{0}}{u_{s}^{0}}=\frac{1}{c}=f^{\prime}(u).

Hence, using the fundamental theorem of calculus on the continuous functions
\gamma_{0}(u)
and
f(u)
, we get

\gamma_{0}(m)=\gamma_{0}(u_{s}^{0})+\int_{u_{s}^{0}}^{m}\gamma_{0}^{\prime}(u)\,du<f(u_{s}^{0})+\int_{u_{s}^{0}}^{m}f^{\prime}(u)\,du=f(m).

Then, the quantity

\theta_{1}:=\frac{f(m)-\gamma_{0}(m)}{4}

is positive and
we have

\gamma_{0}(m)+2\theta_{1}<f(m).

(3.233)

Now, by the uniform convergence of
\gamma_{a}
to
\gamma_{0}

given by Lemma
3.21
,
we know that there exists
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\frac{1}{c}\right)
such that, if
a\in(0,\varepsilon]
,

\underset{u\in[u_{s}^{0},m]}{\sup}|\gamma_{a}(u)-\gamma_{0}(u)|<{\theta_{1}}.

(3.234)

By this and (
3.233
), we obtain that

\gamma_{a}(m)+{\theta_{1}}<f(m)\quad{\mbox{ for all }}a\in(0,\varepsilon].

(3.235)

We remark that formula (
3.235
) will give the desired claim
in (
3.231
) for conveniently small values of
a
.

We are now left with considering the case
a>\varepsilon
.
To this end, recalling (
3.11
), (
3.197
), by the first statement in
Lemma
3.25
, used here with
a^{*}:=\varepsilon
,
we get

\gamma_{a}(u)\leq\gamma_{\varepsilon}(u)\quad\text{for all}\ a>\varepsilon\ \text{and for all}\ u\in[u_{s}^{\varepsilon},u_{d}^{\varepsilon}].

(3.236)

Now we observe that

u^{a}_{d}\geq u_{s}^{\varepsilon}.

(3.237)

Indeed, suppose not, namely

u^{a}_{d}<u_{s}^{\varepsilon}.

(3.238)

Then, by the monotonicity of
\gamma_{a}
, we have that
\gamma_{a}(u^{a}_{d})\leq\gamma_{a}(u_{s}^{\varepsilon})
.
This and (
3.236
) yield that
\gamma_{a}(u^{a}_{d})\leq\gamma_{\varepsilon}(u_{s}^{\varepsilon})
. Hence,
the monotonicity of
\gamma_{\varepsilon}
gives that
\gamma_{a}(u^{a}_{d})\leq\gamma_{\varepsilon}(u_{d}^{\varepsilon})
.
This and (
3.197
) lead to
1-u^{a}_{d}\leq 1-u_{d}^{\varepsilon}
,
that is
u_{d}^{\varepsilon}\leq u^{a}_{d}
. From this inequality,
using again (
3.238
), we deduce that
u_{d}^{\varepsilon}<u_{s}^{\varepsilon}
. This is in contradiction with (
3.201
)
and thus the proof of (
3.237
)
is complete.

We also notice that

u^{a}_{d}\geq u_{d}^{\varepsilon}.

(3.239)

Indeed, suppose not, say

u^{a}_{d}<u_{d}^{\varepsilon}.

(3.240)

Then, by (
3.237
), we have that
u^{a}_{d}\in[u_{s}^{\varepsilon},u_{d}^{\varepsilon}]
and therefore we can apply (
3.236
)
to say that
\gamma_{a}(u^{a}_{d})\leq\gamma_{\varepsilon}(u^{a}_{d})
.
Also, by the monotonicity of
\gamma_{\varepsilon}
,
we have that
\gamma_{\varepsilon}(u^{a}_{d})\leq\gamma_{\varepsilon}(u^{\varepsilon}_{d})
.

With these items of information and (
3.197
), we find that

1-u^{a}_{d}=\gamma_{a}(u^{a}_{d})\leq\gamma_{\varepsilon}(u^{\varepsilon}_{d})=1-u^{\varepsilon}_{d},

and accordingly
u^{a}_{d}\geq u^{\varepsilon}_{d}
.
This is in contradiction with (
3.240
)
and establishes (
3.239
).

Moreover, by (
3.11
) and (
3.14
),
we know that
u_{s}^{0}>u_{s}^{a^{*}}
, for every
a^{*}>0
.
Therefore, setting
\tilde{u}_{d}^{a^{*}}:=\min\{u_{d}^{a^{*}},u_{s}^{0}\}
, we have that
\tilde{u}_{d}^{a^{*}}\in[u_{s}^{a^{*}},u_{d}^{a^{*}}]
. Thus, we are in the position of
using the first statement
in Lemma
3.25
with
a:=\varepsilon

and deduce that

\gamma_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{u}_{d}^{a^{*}})\leq\gamma_{a^{*}}(\tilde{u}_{d}^{a^{*}})\qquad{\mbox{for all}}\quad a^{*}<\varepsilon.

(3.241)

We also remark that

u_{d}^{a^{*}}\to u_{s}^{0}\qquad{\mbox{as }}\;a^{*}\to 0.

(3.242)

Indeed,
up to a subsequence we can assume that
u_{d}^{a^{*}}\to\tilde{u}
as
a^{*}\to 0
, for some
\tilde{u}\in[0,1]
. Also, by (
3.197
),

\gamma_{a^{*}}(u_{d}^{a^{*}})=1-u_{d}^{a^{*}},

and then the uniform convergence of
\gamma_{a^{*}}

in Lemma
3.21
yields that

\gamma_{0}(\tilde{u})=1-\tilde{u}.

This and (
3.197
) lead to
\tilde{u}=u_{d}^{0}
.
Since

u_{d}^{0}=u_{s}^{0}

(3.243)

in virtue of (
3.14
),
we thus conclude that
\tilde{u}=u_{s}^{0}

and the proof of (
3.242
)
is thereby complete.

As a consequence of (
3.242
),
we have that
\tilde{u}_{d}^{a^{*}}\to u_{s}^{0}
as
a^{*}\to 0
. Hence,
using again the uniform convergence of
\gamma_{a^{*}}

in Lemma
3.21
,
we obtain that
\gamma_{a^{*}}(\tilde{u}_{d}^{a^{*}})\to\gamma_{0}(u^{0}_{s})
.
From this and (
3.241
), we conclude that

\gamma_{\varepsilon}(u^{0}_{s})\leq\gamma_{0}(u^{0}_{s}).

(3.244)

Now we claim that

u_{d}^{\varepsilon}>u_{s}^{0}.

(3.245)

Indeed, suppose, by contradiction,
that

u_{d}^{\varepsilon}\leq u_{s}^{0}.

(3.246)

Then, the monotonicity
of
\gamma_{\varepsilon}
, together with (
3.243
)
and (
3.244
), gives that

1-u_{d}^{\varepsilon}=\gamma_{\varepsilon}(u_{d}^{\varepsilon})\leq\gamma_{\varepsilon}(u_{s}^{0})=1-u_{s}^{0}.

From this and (
3.246
) we deduce that
u_{d}^{\varepsilon}=u_{s}^{0}
. In particular, we
have that
u^{0}_{s}\in(u_{s}^{\varepsilon},u^{\varepsilon}_{\mathcal{M}})
.
Accordingly, by (
3.187
),

1-u^{0}_{s}=1-u_{d}^{\varepsilon}=\gamma_{\varepsilon}(u_{d}^{\varepsilon})=\gamma_{\varepsilon}(u^{0}_{s})<\frac{u^{0}_{s}}{{\rho}c}.

As a consequence,

u^{0}_{s}>\frac{\rho c}{1+\rho c},

and this is in contradiction with (
3.14
).
The proof of (
3.245
) is thereby complete.

As a byproduct of (
3.243
)
and (
3.245
), we have that

v^{\varepsilon}_{d}=\gamma_{\varepsilon}(u^{\varepsilon}_{d})=1-u_{d}^{\varepsilon}<1-u_{s}^{0}=1-u_{d}^{0}=\gamma_{0}(u^{0}_{d})=\gamma_{0}(u^{0}_{s})=v^{0}_{s}.

(3.247)

Similarly, by means of (
3.239
),

v^{a}_{d}=\gamma_{a}(u^{a}_{d})=1-u^{a}_{d}\leq 1-u_{d}^{\varepsilon}=\gamma_{\varepsilon}(u_{d}^{\varepsilon})=v_{d}^{\varepsilon}.

(3.248)

In light of (
3.239
), (
3.245
),
(
3.247
) and (
3.248
),
we can write that

1>u_{d}^{a}\geq u_{d}^{\varepsilon}>u_{s}^{0}>0\quad\text{and}\quad 1>v_{s}^{0}>v_{d}^{\varepsilon}\geq v_{d}^{a}>0.

(3.249)

Figure 3.6
:
The figures illustrate the functions involved in the proof of Theorem
3.4
for the case
\rho<1
.
The two vertical lines correspond to the values
u_{d}^{\varepsilon}
and
m
. The thick black line represents the boundary of
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
; the blue line is the graph of
\gamma_{0}(u)
; the dark violet lines delimit the area where
\gamma_{a}(u)
for
a\leq\varepsilon
might be; the red line is the upper limit of
\gamma_{a}(u)
for
a>\varepsilon
. The image was realized using a simulation in Python for the values
\rho=0.35
and
c=1.2
.

Now,
to complete the proof of (
3.231
)
when
a>\varepsilon
,
we consider two cases depending on the order of
m
and
u_{d}^{\varepsilon}
. If
u_{d}^{\varepsilon}\geq m
, by (
3.249
) we have that
m<1
and
f(m)=1
. Then,

\gamma_{a}(m)\leq\gamma_{a}(u_{d}^{\varepsilon})\leq\gamma_{\varepsilon}(u_{d}^{\varepsilon})=v_{d}^{\varepsilon}<1=f(m),

(3.250)

thanks to the monotonicity of
\gamma_{a}
, (
3.236
) and (
3.249
).
We define

\theta_{2}:=\frac{1-v_{d}^{\varepsilon}}{2},

which is positive thanks to (
3.249
). From (
3.250
), we get that

\gamma_{a}(m)+\theta_{2}\leq v_{d}^{\varepsilon}+\theta_{2}<1=f(m).

(3.251)

This formula proves the claim in (
3.231
) for
a>\varepsilon
and
u_{d}^{\varepsilon}\geq m
.

If instead
u_{d}^{\varepsilon}<m
, then we proceed as follows.
By (
3.249
) we have

\gamma_{a}(u_{d}^{a})=v_{d}^{a}\leq v_{d}^{\varepsilon}<v_{s}^{0}=f(u_{s}^{0}).

(3.252)

Now we set

\theta_{3}:=\frac{f(u_{d}^{\varepsilon})-f(u_{s}^{0})}{2}.

Using the definition of
f
in (
3.228
), we see that

\theta_{3}=\frac{u_{d}^{\varepsilon}-u_{s}^{0}}{2c},

and accordingly
\theta_{3}
is positive, due to (
3.249
).

From (
3.252
) we have

\gamma_{a}(u_{d}^{a})+\theta_{3}<f(u_{s}^{0})+\theta_{3}<f(u_{d}^{\varepsilon}).

(3.253)

Now we show that, on any trajectory
(u(t),v(t))
lying
on the graph of
\gamma_{a}
, it holds that

\dot{v}(t)>\frac{\dot{u}(t)}{c}\quad\text{provided that}\ u(t)\in(u_{d}^{a},u^{a}_{\mathcal{M}}).

(3.254)

To prove this, we first observe that
u(t)>u_{d}^{a}>u_{s}^{a}
,
thanks to (
3.201
).
Hence, we can exploit formula (
3.187
) of Lemma
3.24
and get that

\gamma_{a}(u(t))-\frac{u(t)}{\rho c}<0.

(3.255)

Also, by the monotonicity of
\gamma_{a}

and (
3.197
),

\gamma_{a}(u(t))\geq\gamma_{a}(u_{d}^{a})=1-u_{d}^{a}>1-u(t).

From this and (
3.255
) it follows that

\left(\dot{v}(t)-\frac{\dot{u}(t)}{c}\right)=\rho\left(\gamma_{a}(u(t))-\frac{u(t)}{\rho c}\right)(1-u(t)-\gamma_{a}(u(t)))>0

provided that
u(t)\in(u_{d}^{a},u^{a}_{\mathcal{M}})
, and this proves (
3.254
).

In addition, for such a trajectory
(u(t),v(t))
we have that

\begin{split}&\dot{u}(t)=u(t)\,(1-u(t)-\gamma_{a}(u(t))-ac)\\
&\qquad\qquad<u(t)\,(1-u(t)-\gamma_{a}(u_{d}^{a}))=u(t)\,(1-u(t)-1+u_{d}^{a})<0,\end{split}

provided that
u(t)\in(u_{d}^{a},u^{a}_{\mathcal{M}})
.

From this and (
3.254
), we get

\gamma_{a}^{\prime}(u(t))=\frac{\dot{v}(t)}{\dot{u}(t)}<\frac{1}{c}=f^{\prime}(u(t)),

provided that
u(t)\in(u_{d}^{a},u^{a}_{\mathcal{M}})
.

Consequently, taking as initial datum of the trajectory
an arbitrary point
(u,\gamma_{a}(u))
with
u\in(u_{d}^{a},u^{a}_{\mathcal{M}})
, we can write that, for all
u\in(u_{d}^{a},u^{a}_{\mathcal{M}})
,

\gamma_{a}^{\prime}(u)<f^{\prime}(u).

As a result, integrating and using (
3.236
),
for all
u\in(u_{d}^{a},u^{a}_{\mathcal{M}})
, we have

\gamma_{a}(u)=\gamma_{a}(u_{d}^{a})+\int_{u_{d}^{a}}^{u}\gamma_{a}^{\prime}(u)\,du<\gamma_{a}(u_{d}^{a})+\int_{u_{d}^{a}}^{u}f^{\prime}(u)\,du=\gamma_{a}(u_{d}^{a})+f(u)-f(u_{d}^{a}).

Then, making use (
3.253
), for
u\in(u_{d}^{a},u^{a}_{\mathcal{M}})
,

\gamma_{a}(u)+\theta_{3}<\gamma_{a}(u_{d}^{a})+f(u)-f(u_{d}^{a})+\theta_{3}\leq f(u)-f(u_{d}^{a})+f(u_{d}^{\varepsilon}).

(3.256)

Also, recalling (
3.249
)
and the monotonicity of
f
, we see that
f(u_{d}^{\varepsilon})\leq f(u_{d}^{a})
. Combining this and (
3.256
),
we deduce that

\gamma_{a}(u)+\theta_{3}<f(u)\qquad{\mbox{for all }}u\in(u_{d}^{a},u^{a}_{\mathcal{M}}).

(3.257)

We also observe that if
u\in(u_{d}^{\varepsilon},u_{d}^{a}]
,
then the monotonicity of
\gamma_{a}
yields that
\gamma_{a}(u)\leq\gamma_{a}(u_{d}^{a})
. It follows from this and (
3.253
)
that
\gamma_{a}(u)+\theta_{3}<f(u_{d}^{\varepsilon})
.
This and the monotonicity of
f
give that

\gamma_{a}(u)+\theta_{3}<f(u)\qquad{\mbox{for all }}u\in(u_{d}^{\varepsilon},u_{d}^{a}].

Comparing this with (
3.257
),
we obtain

\gamma_{a}(u)+\theta_{3}<f(u)\qquad{\mbox{for all }}u\in(u_{d}^{\varepsilon},u^{a}_{\mathcal{M}})

and
therefore

\gamma_{a}(u)+\theta_{3}\leq f(u)\qquad{\mbox{for all }}u\in[u_{d}^{\varepsilon},u^{a}_{\mathcal{M}}].

(3.258)

Now, in view of (
3.230
), we have that
m\in[u_{d}^{\varepsilon},u^{a}_{\mathcal{M}}]
.
Consequently, we can utilize (
3.258
)
with
u:=m
and find that

\gamma_{a}(m)+\theta_{3}\leq f(m)

(3.259)

which gives (
3.231
) in the case
a>\varepsilon
and
u_{d}^{\varepsilon}\leq m

(say, in this case with
\theta\leq\theta_{3}/2
).

That is, by (
3.235
), (
3.251
) and (
3.259
)
we obtain that (
3.231
) holds true
for

\theta:=\frac{1}{2}\,\min\left\{\theta_{1},\ \theta_{2},\ \theta_{3}\right\}.

If we choose
\bar{v}:=f(m)-\frac{\theta}{2}
we have that

0<\gamma_{a}(m)\leq\bar{v}<f(m)\leq 1.

(3.260)

This completes the proof of Theorem
3.4
when
\rho<1
, in light of
the characterizations of
\mathcal{E}(a)
and
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
from Proposition
3.16
and Theorem
3.3
, respectively.
∎

Now we focus on the case
\rho>1
.

Proof of Theorem
3.4
, case
\rho>1
.

As before, the inclusion
\mathcal{V_{\mathcal{K}}}\subseteq\mathcal{V_{\mathcal{A}}}
is trivial since
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{A}
.
To prove that it is strict, we aim to find a point
(\bar{u},\bar{v})\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
such that
(\bar{u},\bar{v})\notin\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{K}}
.
Thus, we have to prove that there exists
(\bar{u},\bar{v})\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
such that,
for all constant strategies
a>0
, we have that
(\bar{u},\bar{v})\notin\mathcal{E}(a)
.

To this end, using the characterizations given in Proposition
3.16
and Theorem
3.3
, we claim that

\begin{split}&\mbox{there exists a point\penalty\ $(\bar{u},\bar{v})\in[0,1]\times[0,1]$ }\\
&\mbox{satisfying\penalty\ $u_{\infty}\leq\bar{u}\leq u_{\mathcal{M}}^{a}$ and\penalty\ $\gamma_{a}(\bar{u})\leq\bar{v}<\zeta(\bar{u})$ for all\penalty\ $a>0$.}\end{split}

(3.261)

For this, we let

m:=\min\left\{1,\frac{c}{(c+1)^{\frac{\rho-1}{\rho}}}\right\}.

By (
3.18
) one sees that

u_{\infty}<m.

(3.262)

In addition, we point out that

m\leq u_{\mathcal{M}}^{a}.

(3.263)

Indeed, since
m\leq 1
, if
u_{\mathcal{M}}^{a}=1
the desired inequality is obvious. If instead
u_{\mathcal{M}}^{a}<1
we have that
(u_{\mathcal{M}}^{a},1)\times(0,1)\subseteq{\mathcal{E}}(a)\subseteq\mathcal{V_{\mathcal{K}}}\subseteq\mathcal{V_{\mathcal{A}}}
. Hence, by (
3.17
),
it follows that
\frac{c}{(c+1)^{\frac{\rho-1}{\rho}}}\leq u_{\mathcal{M}}^{a}
,
which leads to (
3.263
), as desired.

Now we claim that there exists
\theta>0
such that

\gamma_{a}(m)+\theta<\zeta(m)\quad{\mbox{for all }}\;a>0.

(3.264)

We first show some preliminary facts for
\gamma_{a}(u)
.
For all
a>0
, we have that
\mathcal{E}(a)\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
. Owing
to the characterization of
\mathcal{E}(a)
from Proposition
3.16
and of
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{A}}
from Theorem
3.3
(which can be used here, thanks
to (
3.262
) and (
3.263
)), we get that

\gamma_{a}(u)\leq\frac{u}{c}\quad\text{for all }\ u\in(0,u_{\infty}]\ \text{ and }\ a>0.

(3.265)

This is true in particular for
u=u_{\infty}
.

We choose

\delta\in\left(0,\frac{\rho-1}{c}\right)\quad{\mbox{ and }}\quad M:=\max\left\{\frac{1}{c},\frac{\rho+\frac{1}{c}+\delta}{\delta cu_{\infty}}\right\},

(3.266)

and we prove (
3.264
) by treating separately the cases
a>M
and
a\in(0,M]
.

We first consider the case
a>M
.
We let
(u(t),v(t))
be a trajectory for (
3.1
) lying on
\gamma_{a}
and we show that

\dot{v}(t)-\left(\frac{1}{c}+\delta\right)\dot{u}(t)>0\quad\text{provided that }\ u(t)>u_{\infty}\ {\mbox{ and }}\ a>M.

(3.267)

To check this, we observe that

\displaystyle\dot{v}(t)-\left(\frac{1}{c}+\delta\right)\dot{u}(t)

\displaystyle=\left[\rho\gamma_{a}(u(t))-\left(\frac{1}{c}+\delta\right)u(t)\right](1-u(t)-\gamma_{a}(u(t)))+\delta acu(t)

\displaystyle\geq-\left|\rho+\frac{1}{c}+\delta\right|+\delta acu_{\infty}>0,

where the last inequality is true thanks to the hypothesis
a>M
and the definition of
M

in (
3.266
). This proves (
3.267
).

Moreover, for
a>M\geq\frac{1}{c}
we have
\dot{u}<0
.
From this, (
3.267
) and the invariance of
\gamma_{a}
for the flow, we get

\gamma_{a}^{\prime}(u(t))=\frac{\dot{v}(t)}{\dot{u}(t)}<\frac{1}{c}+\delta,

(3.268)

provided that
u(t)>u_{\infty}
and
a>M
.

For this reason and (
3.265
), we get

\gamma_{a}(u(t))=\gamma_{a}(u_{\infty})+\int_{u_{\infty}}^{u(t)}\gamma_{a}^{\prime}(\tau)\,d\tau\leq\frac{u_{\infty}}{c}+\left(\frac{1}{c}+\delta\right)(u(t)-u_{\infty})

(3.269)

provided that
u(t)>u_{\infty}
and
a>M
.

Furthermore, thanks to the choice of
\delta
in (
3.266
), we have

\zeta^{\prime}(u)=\frac{\rho u^{\rho-1}}{cu_{\infty}^{\rho-1}}>\frac{\rho}{c}>\frac{1}{c}+\delta\quad\text{for all }\ u>u_{\infty}.

Since also
\zeta(u_{\infty})=\frac{u_{\infty}}{c}
, by (
3.269
) we deduce that

\gamma_{a}(u(t))\leq\frac{u_{\infty}}{c}+\left(\frac{1}{c}+\delta\right)(u(t)-u_{\infty})<\zeta(u_{\infty})+\int_{u_{\infty}}^{u(t)}\zeta^{\prime}(\tau)\,d\tau=\zeta(u(t)),

(3.270)

provided that
u(t)>u_{\infty}
and
a>M
.

In particular, given any
u>u_{\infty}
, we can take a trajectory starting at
(u,\gamma_{a}(u))

and deduce from (
3.270
) that

\gamma_{a}(u)\leq\frac{u_{\infty}}{c}+\left(\frac{1}{c}+\delta\right)(u-u_{\infty})<\zeta(u_{\infty})+\int_{u_{\infty}}^{u}\zeta^{\prime}(\tau)\,d\tau=\zeta(u),

whenever
a>M
. We stress that, in light of (
3.262
), we can take
u:=m

in the above chain of inequalities, concluding that

\gamma_{a}(m)\leq\frac{u_{\infty}}{c}+\left(\frac{1}{c}+\delta\right)(m-u_{\infty})<\zeta(m).

We rewrite this in the form

\gamma_{a}(m)\leq\left(\frac{1}{c}+\delta\right)m-{\delta}u_{\infty}<\zeta(m).

(3.271)

We define

\theta_{1}:=\frac{1}{2}\left[\zeta(m)-\left(\frac{1}{c}+\delta\right)m+{\delta}u_{\infty}\right],

(3.272)

that is positive thanks to the last inequality in (
3.271
). Then by the first inequality in (
3.271
) we have

\gamma_{a}(m)+\theta_{1}\leq\left(\frac{1}{c}+\delta\right)m-{\delta}u_{\infty}+\theta_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(\frac{1}{c}+\delta\right)m-{{\delta}u_{\infty}}\right]+\frac{\zeta(m)}{2}.

Hence, using again the last inequality in (
3.271
), we obtain that

\gamma_{a}(m)+\theta_{1}<\zeta(m),

(3.273)

which gives the claim in (
3.264
) for the case
a>M
.

Now we treat the case
a\in(0,M]
.
We claim that

u_{d}^{M}>u_{\infty}.

(3.274)

Here, we are using the notation
u_{d}^{M}
to denote the point
u_{d}^{a}
when
a:=M
. To prove (
3.274
) we argue as follows.
Since
M\geq\frac{1}{c}
, by Propositions
3.8
and
3.14
we have

\gamma_{M}^{\prime}(0)=\frac{M}{\rho-1+Mc}<\frac{1}{c}.

(3.275)

Moreover, since the graph of
\gamma_{M}(u)
is a parametrization of a trajectory for (
3.1
) with
a=M
, we have that
\dot{v}(t)=\gamma_{M}^{\prime}(u(t))\dot{u}(t)
.
Hence, at all points
(\bar{u},\bar{v})
with
\bar{u}\in(0,u_{\infty})
and
\bar{v}=\gamma_{M}(\bar{u})
we have

\gamma_{M}^{\prime}(\bar{u})=\frac{M\bar{u}-\rho\bar{v}(1-\bar{u}-\bar{v})}{Mc\bar{u}-\bar{u}(1-\bar{u}-\bar{v})}.

(3.276)

We stress that the denominator in the right hand side of (
3.276
)
is strictly positive, since
M\geq\frac{1}{c}
and
\bar{u}>0
.

In addition, we have that

\begin{split}\frac{1}{c}-\frac{M\bar{u}-\rho\bar{v}(1-\bar{u}-\bar{v})}{Mc\bar{u}-\bar{u}(1-\bar{u}-\bar{v})}=\frac{(\rho c\bar{v}-\bar{u})(1-\bar{u}-\bar{v})}{Mc^{2}\bar{u}-c\bar{u}(1-\bar{u}-\bar{v})}.\end{split}

(3.277)

Also,

u_{s}^{M}=0<\bar{u}<u_{\infty}<m\leq u^{M}_{\mathcal{M}},

thanks to (
3.262
) and (
3.263
).
Hence, we can exploit formula (
3.189
) in Lemma
3.24

with the strict inequality, thus obtaining that

\rho c\bar{v}-\bar{u}=\rho c\gamma_{M}(\bar{u})-\bar{u}>0.

(3.278)

Moreover, by (
3.265
),

1-\bar{u}-\bar{v}=1-\bar{u}-\gamma_{M}(\bar{u})\geq 1-\bar{u}-\frac{\bar{u}}{c}>1-u_{\infty}-\frac{u_{\infty}}{c}=0.

Therefore, using the latter estimate and (
3.278
)
into (
3.277
), we get that

\begin{split}\frac{1}{c}-\frac{M\bar{u}-\rho\bar{v}(1-\bar{u}-\bar{v})}{Mc\bar{u}-\bar{u}(1-\bar{u}-\bar{v})}>0.\end{split}

From this and (
3.276
), we have that

\gamma_{M}^{\prime}(u)<\frac{1}{c}\quad\text{for all}\ u\in(0,u_{\infty}).

This, together with (
3.275
) and the fact that
\gamma_{M}(0)=0
, gives

\gamma_{M}(u)=\gamma_{M}(u)-\gamma_{M}(0)=\int_{0}^{u}\gamma_{M}^{\prime}(\tau)\,d\tau<\frac{u}{c}

for all
u\in(0,u_{\infty}]
.
This inequality yields that

\gamma_{M}(u_{\infty})<\frac{u_{\infty}}{c}=1-u_{\infty}.

(3.279)
