.

The examples provided here show that the “abstract”
structural hypothesis (
4.2
) is reasonable and can be explicitly
checked in several cases of interest.
We are also confident that other interesting
examples fulfilling such an assumption
can be found, therefore Theorem
4.1
turns out to play a pivotal
role in the asymptotics of real and complex valued,
possibly nonlinear, and possibly fractional, operators.

Comparison with the existing literature

In general, in problems of the type (
4.1
) it is very difficult to provide
explicit solutions and often the system has no unique solution, see e.g.
[
25
]
. Therefore, even partial information on the solutions is important.

In the case of a Kirchhoff parabolic equation with purely classical time-derivative in the degenerate case
m(0)=0
, Ghisi and Gobbino
[
56
]
found the time-decay estimate

c(1+t)^{-1}\leq\|\nabla u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq C(1+t)^{-1}\qquad{\mbox{for all }}t>0.

(4.17)

for some costants
C,c>0
depending on initial data. From this,
performing an integration of the gradient along paths
4
4

4

More precisely,
the fact that (
4.17
) implies (
4.19
)
can be seen as a consequence of the following observation:
for every
u\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
,

\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\,\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

(4.18)

where
C>0
depends on
n
and
\Omega
.
Indeed,
fix
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
B_{1}(x_{0})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\Omega

and
\Omega\subset B_{R}(x_{0})
, for some
R>1
.
Then, for every
x\in\Omega
we have that
|x-x_{0}|\in[1,R]
and thus

\displaystyle|u(x)|^{2}=|u(x)-u(x_{0})|^{2}=\left|\int_{0}^{1}\nabla u(x_{0}+t(x-x_{0}))\cdot(x-x_{0})\,dt\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq|x-x_{0}|^{2}\,\int_{0}^{1}|\nabla u(x_{0}+t(x-x_{0}))|^{2}\,dt\leq R^{2}\,\int_{0}^{1}|\nabla u(x_{0}+t(x-x_{0}))|^{2}\,dt.

On the other hand, if
t\in[0,1/R)
we have that

\big|t(x-x_{0})\big|<\frac{|x-x_{0}|}{R}\leq 1

and so
x_{0}+t(x-x_{0})\in B_{1}(x_{0})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\Omega
,
which in turn implies that
\nabla u(x_{0}+t(x-x_{0}))=0
. This gives that

|u(x)|^{2}\leq R^{2}\,\int_{1/R}^{1}|\nabla u(x_{0}+t(x-x_{0}))|^{2}\,dt.

Hence, using the substitution
x\mapsto y:=x_{0}+t(x-x_{0})
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{2}\,dx\leq R^{2}\,\int_{1/R}^{1}\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla u(x_{0}+t(x-x_{0}))|^{2}\,dx\right]\,dt=R^{2}\,\int_{1/R}^{1}\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla u(y)|^{2}\,\frac{dy}{t^{n}}\right]\,dt

\displaystyle\qquad\leq R^{n+2}\,\int_{1/R}^{1}\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla u(y)|^{2}\,dy\right]\,dt\leq R^{n+2}\,\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=R^{n+2}\,\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

which proves (
4.18
).
, one can find the estimate

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C(1+t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\qquad{\mbox{for all }}t>0.

(4.19)

The latter is exactly the estimate we found
in Theorem
4.4
as a particular case of our analysis.

The fractional porous medium equation with classical derivative has been studied
by Biler, Karch and Imbert
in
[
25
]
, establishing some decay estimates of the
L^{s}
norm, such as

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}\leq t^{-\frac{n}{n+2-2\sigma}\left(1-\frac{1}{s}\right)}.

(4.20)

As a matter of fact, this decay
is slower than what we find in Theorem
4.3
, which is asymptotic to
t^{-1}

(in this sense, Theorem
4.3
here
can be seen as an improvement of the estimates
in
[
25
]
).

On the other hand, in
[
25
]
the Authors also provide a
weak
solution that has exactly the decay in (
4.20
),
thus showing the optimality of (
4.20
)
in this generality,
while our result holds for
strong
solutions. Then, comparing Theorem
4.3
here with the results in (
4.20
)
we obtain that a better decay is valid for regular solutions with respect to the one which is valid
also for irregular ones.

4.2
Proofs

This section contains the proofs of our main results. We start with the proof of Theorem
4.1
.

In order to prove Theorem
4.1
, we need a comparison result for the equation involving the mixed time-derivative. As a matter of fact,
comparison results
for the case of the Caputo derivative are available
in the literature, see e.g. Lemma 2.6 of
[
119
]
. In our arguments
we employ the differentiability of
u
and the fact that
u
is a strong solution, and we obtain:

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
T\in(0,+\infty)\cup\{+\infty\}

and
w,\ v:[0,T)\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
be two Lipschitz continuous
functions.
Assume that
w
is a supersolution and
v
is a subsolution at each differentiability point for the equation

\lambda_{1}\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u(t)+\lambda_{2}\partial_{t}u(t)=-ku^{\gamma}(t)

(4.21)

with
\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}
,
\gamma
,
k>0
.

Then:
if

w(0)>v(0),

(4.22)

we have that

w(t)>v(t)\qquad{\mbox{ for all }}t\in(0,T).

(4.23)

Proof.

By contradiction, let us suppose that for some time
t\in(0,T)
we have
w(t)=v(t)
, and let us call
\tau
the first time for which the equality is reached. Then,
since
w
is a supersolution and
v
is a subsolution of (
4.21
),
we obtain that

\lambda_{1}\partial_{t}^{\alpha}(w-v)(\tau)+\lambda_{2}\partial_{t}(w-v)(\tau)\geq-k[w^{\gamma}(\tau)-v^{\gamma}(\tau)]=0.

(4.24)

Now we distinguish two cases, depending on whether or not
w-v
is differentiable at
\tau
.
To start with, suppose that
w-v
is differentiable at
\tau
.
Since
w\geq v
in
(0,\tau)
, we have that

\partial_{t}(w-v)(\tau)\leq 0.

From this and (
4.24
), we obtain that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{\alpha}(w-v)(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(w-v)(\tau)-(w-v)(0)}{\tau^{\alpha}}+\alpha\int_{0}^{\tau}\frac{(w-v)(\tau)-(w-v)(\rho)}{(\tau-\rho)^{1+\alpha}}d\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{(w-v)(0)}{\tau^{\alpha}}-\alpha\int_{0}^{\tau}\frac{(w-v)(\rho)}{(\tau-\rho)^{1+\alpha}}d\rho

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle-\frac{(w-v)(0)}{\tau^{\alpha}}.

This is in contradiction with (
4.22
) and so it proves (
4.23
) in this case.

Now we focus on the case in which
w-v
is not differentiable at
\tau
.
Then, there exists a sequence
t_{j}\in(0,\tau)

such that
w-v
is differentiable at
t_{j}
, with
\partial_{t}(w-v)(t_{j})\leq 0

and
t_{j}\to\tau
as
j\to+\infty
.
Consequently,
since
w
is a supersolution and
v
is a subsolution of (
4.21
),
we obtain that

\begin{split}&\frac{(w-v)(t_{j})-(w-v)(0)}{t_{j}^{\alpha}}+\alpha\int_{0}^{t_{j}}\frac{(w-v)(t_{j})-(w-v)(\rho)}{(t_{j}-\rho)^{1+\alpha}}d\rho\\
=\;&\partial_{t}^{\alpha}(w-v)(t_{j})\\
\geq\;&\partial_{t}^{\alpha}(w-v)(t_{j})+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}}\partial_{t}(w-v)(t_{j})\\
\geq\;&-\frac{k}{\lambda_{1}}\,[w^{\gamma}(t_{j})-v^{\gamma}(t_{j})].\end{split}

(4.25)

Now we observe that if
f
is a Lipschitz function and
t_{j}\to\tau>0

as
j\to+\infty
, then

\lim_{j\to+\infty}\int_{0}^{t_{j}}\frac{f(t_{j})-f(\rho)}{(t_{j}-\rho)^{1+\alpha}}d\rho=\int_{0}^{\tau}\frac{f(\tau)-f(\rho)}{(\tau-\rho)^{1+\alpha}}d\rho.

(4.26)

To check this, let

F_{j}(\rho):=\chi_{(0,t_{j})}(\rho)\,\frac{f(t_{j})-f(\rho)}{(t_{j}-\rho)^{1+\alpha}},

and let
E\subset(0,+\infty)
be a measurable set, with measure
|E|
less than
a given
\delta>0
. Let also
q:=\frac{1+\alpha}{2\alpha}>1

and denote by
p
its conjugated exponent. Then, by Hölder inequality, for large
j

we have that

\displaystyle\int_{E}|F_{j}(\rho)|\,d\rho

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|E|^{1/p}\,\left(\int_{0}^{+\infty}|F_{j}(\rho)|^{q}\,d\rho\right)^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\delta^{1/p}\,\left(\int_{0}^{t_{j}}\frac{|f(t_{j})-f(\rho)|^{q}}{(t_{j}-\rho)^{(1+\alpha)q}}\,d\rho\right)^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle L\,\delta^{1/p}\,\left(\int_{0}^{t_{j}}\frac{d\rho}{(t_{j}-\rho)^{\alpha q}}\right)^{1/q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle L\,\delta^{1/p}\,\left(\int_{0}^{t_{j}}\frac{d\rho}{(t_{j}-\rho)^{(1+\alpha)/2}}\right)^{1/q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle L\,\delta^{1/p}\,\left(\frac{2t_{j}^{(1-\alpha)/2}}{1-\alpha}\right)^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle L\,\left(\frac{2(\tau+1)^{(1-\alpha)/2}}{1-\alpha}\right)^{1/q}\,\delta^{1/p},

where
L
is the Lipschitz constant of
f
. Consequently, by the
Vitali Convergence Theorem,
we obtain that

\lim_{j\to+\infty}\int_{0}^{+\infty}F_{j}(\rho)\,d\rho=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\lim_{j\to+\infty}F_{j}(\rho)\,d\rho,

which gives (
4.26
), as desired.

Now, we take the limit as
j\to+\infty
in (
4.25
),
exploiting (
4.26
) and the fact that
w(\tau)=v(\tau)
. In this
way, we have that

-\frac{(w-v)(0)}{\tau^{\alpha}}-\alpha\int_{0}^{\tau}\frac{(w-v)(\rho)}{(\tau-\rho)^{1+\alpha}}d\rho\geq 0.

Since
w\geq v
in
(0,\tau)
, the latter inequality implies that

-\frac{(w-v)(0)}{\tau^{\alpha}}\geq 0.

This is in contradiction with (
4.22
) and so it completes the proof of (
4.23
).
∎

It is also useful to observe that Lemma
4.8

holds true also for the classical derivative (i.e. when
\lambda_{1}=0
).
We give its statement and proof for the sake of completeness:

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
T\in(0,+\infty)\cup\{+\infty\}
,

w,\ v:[0,T)\rightarrow[0,+\infty)
be two Lipschitz continuous
functions.
Assume that
w
is a supersolution and
v
is a subsolution at each differentiability point for the equation

\partial_{t}u(t)=-ku^{\gamma}(t)

(4.27)

with
\gamma
,
k>0
.

Then:
if

w(0)>v(0),

(4.28)

we have that

w(t)>v(t)\qquad{\mbox{ for all }}t\in(0,T).

(4.29)

Proof.

Suppose that (
4.29
) is false. Then there exists
\tau\in(0,T)

such that
w>v
in
(0,\tau)
and

w(\tau)=v(\tau).

(4.30)

We fix
\varepsilon>0
, to be taken as small as we wish in the sequel,
and define

f(t):=w(t)-v(t)+\varepsilon\,(t-\tau).

(4.31)

We observe that

f(0)=w(0)-v(0)-\varepsilon\tau\geq\frac{w(0)-v(0)}{2}>0,

as long as
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small,
and
f(\tau)=w(\tau)-v(\tau)=0
.
Therefore there exists
\tau_{\varepsilon}\in(0,\tau]
such that

f>0
in
(0,\tau_{\varepsilon})

and
f(\tau_{\varepsilon})=0
.

(4.32)

We claim that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\tau_{\varepsilon}=\tau.

(4.33)

Indeed, suppose, by contradiction, that, up to a subsequence,
\tau_{\varepsilon}

converges to some
\tau_{0}\in[0,\tau)
as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
. Then we have that

0=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}f(\tau_{\varepsilon})=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}w(\tau_{\varepsilon})-v(\tau_{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon\,(\tau_{\varepsilon}-\tau)=w(\tau_{0})-v(\tau_{0}).

This is in contradiction with the definition of
\tau

and so (
4.33
) is proved.

Now, from (
4.32
), we know that
there exists a sequence
t_{j}\in(0,\tau_{\varepsilon}]
such that
f

is differentiable at
t_{j}
,
\partial_{t}f(t_{j})\leq 0

and
t_{j}\to\tau_{\varepsilon}
as
j\to+\infty
.

Accordingly, we deduce from (
4.27
) and (
4.31
) that

0\geq\partial_{t}f(t_{j})=\partial_{t}(w-v)(t_{j})+\varepsilon\geq-k\big(w^{\gamma}(t_{j})-v^{\gamma}(t_{j})\big)+\varepsilon.

Hence, taking the limit as
j\to+\infty
,

\frac{\varepsilon}{k}\leq w^{\gamma}(\tau_{\varepsilon})-v^{\gamma}(\tau_{\varepsilon})=\big(v(\tau_{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon\,(\tau-\tau_{\varepsilon})\big)^{\gamma}-v^{\gamma}(\tau_{\varepsilon}).

(4.34)

We claim that

\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}v(\tau_{\varepsilon})>0.

(4.35)

Indeed, if not, by (
4.30
) and (
4.33
),

0=\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}v(\tau_{\varepsilon})=v(\tau)=w(\tau).

(4.36)

We observe that this implies that

\gamma\in(0,1).

(4.37)

Indeed, since
w
is a supersolution of (
4.27
),
we have that

\displaystyle w(t)\geq w(0)\,e^{-kt},\qquad{\mbox{when }}\gamma=1

and

\displaystyle w(t)\geq\frac{1}{\left(\frac{1}{w^{\gamma-1}(0)}+k(\gamma-1)t\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}},\qquad{\mbox{when }}\gamma>1,

as long as
w(t)>0
, and so for all
t>0
.
In particular, we have that
w(\tau)>0
, in contradiction with (
4.36
),
and this proves (
4.37
).

Then, we use that
v
is a subsolution of (
4.27
) and (
4.36
)
to write that, for any
t\in(0,\tau)
,

-\frac{v^{1-\gamma}(t)}{1-\gamma}=\frac{v^{1-\gamma}(\tau)-v^{1-\gamma}(t)}{1-\gamma}=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\int_{t}^{\tau}\partial_{\rho}(v^{1-\gamma}(\rho))\,d\rho=\int_{t}^{\tau}\frac{\partial_{t}v(\rho)}{v^{\gamma}(\rho)}\,d\rho\leq-k(\tau-t).

Therefore, recalling (
4.37
),

v^{1-\gamma}(t)\geq k(1-\gamma)(\tau-t),

and thus

v(t)=v(t)\geq\big(k(1-\gamma)(\tau-t)\big)^{1/(1-\gamma)}.

(4.38)

Similarly, using that
w
is a supersolution of (
4.27
) and (
4.36
)
we obtain that, for any
t\in(0,\tau)
,

w(t)\leq\big(k(1-\gamma)(\tau-t)\big)^{1/(1-\gamma)}.

Comparing this and (
4.38
), we conclude that

w(0)\leq\big(k(1-\gamma)\tau\big)^{1/(1-\gamma)}\leq v(0),

which is in contradiction with (
4.28
), and so the proof of (
4.35
)
is complete.

Then, using (
4.34
)
and (
4.35
), a Taylor expansion gives that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{v^{\gamma}(\tau_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon}\,\left[\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon\,(\tau-\tau_{\varepsilon})}{v(\tau_{\varepsilon})}\right)^{\gamma}-1\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{v^{\gamma}(\tau_{\varepsilon})}{\varepsilon}\left[\frac{\gamma\varepsilon\,(\tau-\tau_{\varepsilon})}{v(\tau_{\varepsilon})}+O\left(\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\,(\tau-\tau_{\varepsilon})^{2}}{v^{2}(\tau_{\varepsilon})}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma\,(\tau-\tau_{\varepsilon})}{v^{1-\gamma}(\tau_{\varepsilon})}+O\left(\frac{\varepsilon\,(\tau-\tau_{\varepsilon})^{2}}{v^{2-\gamma}(\tau_{\varepsilon})}\right).

Then, sending
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
and recalling (
4.33
)
and (
4.35
), we conclude that
\frac{1}{k}\leq 0
.
This is a contradiction and the proof of (
4.29
) is thereby complete.
∎

With this preliminary work, we are in the position of proving the general
claim stated in Theorem
4.1
.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

First, notice that

{\partial_{t}|u|^{s}}=s|u|^{s-1}\left(\frac{\Re(u)\partial_{t}\Re(u)+\Im(u)\partial_{t}\Im(u)}{|u|}\right)=s|u|^{s-2}\Re\{\bar{u}\,\partial_{t}u\}.

(4.39)

Using (
4.39
) and exchanging the order of the integral and the derivative, we have

\begin{split}\int_{\Omega}|u|^{s-2}\Re\{\bar{u}\,\partial_{t}u\}\;dx&=\int_{\Omega}\frac{\partial_{t}|u|^{s}}{s}\;dx=\frac{1}{s}\partial_{t}\int_{\Omega}|u|^{s}\;dx=\frac{1}{s}\partial_{t}\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}^{s}\\
&=\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}^{s-1}\partial_{t}\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}.\end{split}

(4.40)

Now we claim that

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}^{s-1}\partial_{t}^{\alpha}(\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)})\leq\int_{\Omega}|u(x,t)|^{s-2}\Re\{\bar{u}(x,t)\partial_{t}^{\alpha}(u(x,t))\}\,dx.

(4.41)

This formula is similar to one given in Corollary 3.1 of
[
119
]

for general kernels. In our setting,
we provide
an easier proof for the case of the Caputo derivative, comprising also the case
of complex valued operators.
To prove (
4.41
),
using the definition of Caputo derivative we see that

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}&|u(x,t)|^{s-2}\Re\{\bar{u}(x,t)\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u(x,t)\}\;dx\\
&=\int_{\Omega}|u(x,t)|^{s-2}\Re\left\{\bar{u}(x,t)\left[\dfrac{u(x,t)-u(x,0)}{t^{\alpha}}+\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\dfrac{u(x,t)-u(x,\tau)}{(t-\tau)^{1+\alpha}}\;d\tau\right]\right\}\;dx\\
&=\int_{\Omega}|u(x,t)|^{s-2}\bigg(\frac{|u(x,t)|^{2}-\Re\{\bar{u}(x,t)u(x,0)\}}{t^{\alpha}}\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\frac{|u(x,t)|^{2}-\Re\{\bar{u}(x,t)u(x,\tau)\}}{(t-\tau)^{1+\alpha}}\;d\tau\bigg)dx.\end{split}

Hence, by using the Hölder inequality, we get

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}&|u(x,t)|^{s-2}\Re\{\bar{u}(x,t)\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u(x,t)\}\;dx\\
&\geq\frac{\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}^{s}-\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}^{s-1}\|u(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}}{t^{\alpha}}+\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}^{s}}{(t-\tau)^{1+\alpha}}\;d\tau\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt-\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\dfrac{\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}^{s-1}\|u(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}}{(t-\tau)^{1+\alpha}}\;d\tau\\
&=\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}^{s-1}\bigg[\dfrac{\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}-\|u(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}}{t^{\alpha}}\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\dfrac{\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}-\|u(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}}{(t-\tau)^{1+\alpha}}\;d\tau\bigg]\\
&=\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}^{s-1}\partial_{t}^{\alpha}\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}.\end{split}

This completes the proof of (
4.41
).

Now, to make the notation simpler, we set
v(t):=\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}
. By combining (
4.40
) and (
4.41
), we find that

v^{s-1}(t)\left(\lambda_{1}\partial_{t}^{\alpha}v(t)+\lambda_{2}\partial_{t}v(t)\right)\leq\int_{\Omega}|u|^{s-2}(x,t)\Re\left\{\bar{u}(x,t)\left(\lambda_{1}\partial_{t}^{\alpha}u(x,t)+\lambda_{2}\partial_{t}u(x,t)\right)\right\}dx

and so, using the fact that
u
is a solution of (
4.1
), we conclude that

v^{s-1}(t)\left(\lambda_{1}\partial_{t}^{\alpha}v(t)+\lambda_{2}\partial_{t}v(t)\right)\leq-\int_{\Omega}|u|^{s-2}(x,t)\Re\{\bar{u}(x,t)\mathcal{N}[u](x,t)\}dx.

From this, we use the structural hypothesis (
4.2
) and we obtain that

v^{s-1}(t)\left(\lambda_{1}\partial_{t}^{\alpha}v(t)+\lambda_{2}\partial_{t}v(t)\right)\leq-\frac{v^{s-1+\gamma}(t)}{C}.

Hence, we have established
the claim in (
4.3
)
for all
t>0
such that
v(t)>0
.
Then, suppose that for some
\bar{t}>0
we have
v(\bar{t})=0
. Since
v
is nonnegative,
it follows that

\partial_{t}v(\bar{t})=0.

(4.42)

On the other hand, if
v(t)=0
, then

{\partial_{t}^{\alpha}v(t)\leq 0},

(4.43)

because

\partial_{t}^{\alpha}v(t)=\frac{v(t)-v(0)}{t^{\alpha}}+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{v(t)-v(\tau)}{(t-\tau)^{1+\alpha}}d\tau\leq-\frac{v(0)}{t^{\alpha}}-\int_{0}^{t}\frac{v(\tau)}{(t-\tau)^{1+\alpha}}d\tau\leq 0.

So, by (
4.42
) and (
4.43
),

\left(\lambda_{1}\partial_{t}^{\alpha}v(\bar{t})+\lambda_{2}\partial_{t}v(\bar{t})\right)\leq 0
, which gives (
4.3
) also in this case, as desired.

Now we exhibit a supersolution
w(t)
of the equation
(\lambda_{1}\partial_{t}^{\alpha}+\lambda_{2}\partial_{t})v(t)=-\nu v^{\gamma}(t)
, where
\nu:=\frac{1}{C}
.
For this, we recall
Section 7 of
[
119
]
, and we have that the function

w(t):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}u_{0}&{\mbox{if }}t\in[0,t_{0}],\\
Kt^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}&{\mbox{if }}t\geq t_{0},\end{array}\right.

with
K:=u_{0}t_{0}^{\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}
is a supersolution of
\partial_{t}^{\alpha}w(t)=-\nu w^{\gamma}(t)

as long as

t_{0}\geq\dfrac{u_{0}^{1-\gamma}}{\nu}\left(\frac{2^{\alpha}}{\Gamma(1-\alpha)}+\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}\frac{2^{\alpha+\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}\right).

We claim that
\partial_{t}w(t)\geq-\nu w^{\gamma}(t)
.
To prove this, it is equivalent to check that

\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}u_{0}\,t_{0}^{\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\,t^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}-1}\leq\nu\,u_{0}^{\gamma}\,t_{0}^{{\alpha}}\,t^{-\alpha},

which is in turn equivalent to

\frac{\alpha}{\gamma\,\nu}u_{0}^{1-\gamma}\,t_{0}^{\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}-\alpha}\leq t^{1+\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}-\alpha},

and the latter equation holds if

t_{0}\geq\max\left\{1,\frac{\alpha}{\gamma\nu}u_{0}^{1-\gamma}\right\}.

Therefore for
t_{0}
big enough we have that
w(t)
is a supersolution of the equation
(\lambda_{1}\partial_{t}^{\alpha}+\lambda_{2}\partial_{t})v(t)=-\nu v^{\gamma}(t)
. Also,
w(t)
satisfies

w(t)\leq\frac{c}{1+t^{\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}}

for some
c>0
depending only on
\nu,\ \gamma,\ \alpha
and
w(0)
. Hence by the comparison principle in Lemma
4.8
, we infer that
v(t)\leq w(t)
, which
completes the proof of the desired result in (
4.4
). ∎

Proof of Theorem
4.2
.

The proof is identical to the one of Theorem
4.1
a
part from the construction of the supersolution
(and from the use of the comparison principle in Lemma
4.9

rather than in Lemma
4.8
). Our aim is now to find a supersolution to the equation (
4.3
) in the case
\lambda_{1}=0
, that we can write as

v^{\prime}(t)=-\frac{1}{C}v^{\gamma}(t)

(4.44)

where
C
is the constant given in the hypothesis. To construct this supersolution,
we distinguish the cases
0<\gamma\leq 1

and
\gamma>1
.

We define

w_{0}:=\|u_{0}(\cdot)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)},

(4.45)

t_{0}:=\left\{\begin{matrix}0&{\mbox{if }}\gamma=1,\\
\max\left\{0,\ \frac{C}{1-\gamma}(w_{0}^{1-\gamma}-1)\right\}&{\mbox{if }}0<\gamma<1,\end{matrix}\right.

(4.46)

and

\theta_{0}=\left(w_{0}-\dfrac{(1-\gamma)}{C}t_{0}\right).

(4.47)

Notice that, for
0<\gamma<1

\theta_{0}\leq 1.

(4.48)

In fact,

\frac{C}{1-\gamma}(w_{0}^{1-\gamma}-1)\leq t_{0}

implies

\left(w_{0}^{1-\gamma}-\frac{(1-\gamma)}{C}t_{0}\right)\leq 1

and that proves (
4.48
).
Then, we see that the function

w(t):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\left(w_{0}^{1-\gamma}-\dfrac{(1-\gamma)t}{C}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\gamma}},&{\mbox{if }}t\in[0,t_{0}]\\
\theta_{0}\,e^{\frac{t_{0}-t}{C}},&{\mbox{if }}t\in(t_{0},+\infty)\end{array}\right.

(4.49)

is a continuous and Lipschitz function, moreover it is a solution of (
4.44
)
in the case
\gamma=1
and a supersolution of (
4.44
) in the case
0<\gamma<1
. Indeed, to check this, we observe that, for
t\in[0,t_{0}]
,

\displaystyle\hskip-10.00002ptw^{\prime}(t)+\frac{1}{C}w^{\gamma}(t))

\displaystyle\hskip 30.00005pt=-\dfrac{1}{C}\left(w_{0}^{1-\gamma}-\dfrac{(1-\gamma)t}{C}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}}+\dfrac{1}{C}\left(w_{0}^{1-\gamma}-\dfrac{(1-\gamma)t}{C}\right)^{\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}}

\displaystyle\hskip 30.00005pt=0,

while
for all
t>t_{0}
,

\displaystyle C\left(w^{\prime}(t)+\frac{1}{C}w^{\gamma}(t)\right)=-\theta_{0}e^{\frac{(t_{0}-t)}{C}}+\theta_{0}^{\gamma}e^{\frac{\gamma(t_{0}-t)}{C}}=\theta_{0}^{\gamma}e^{\frac{\gamma(t_{0}-t)}{C}}\left(1-\theta_{0}^{1-\gamma}e^{\frac{(1-\gamma)(t_{0}-t)}{C}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\theta_{0}^{\gamma}e^{\frac{\gamma(t_{0}-t)}{C}}\left(1-\theta_{0}^{1-\gamma}\right)\geq 0,

where the inequality holds thanks to (
4.48
).
Notice also that the function
w
is Lipschitz since it is piecewise continuous and derivable and it is continuous in the point
t=t_{0}
because of the definition of
\theta
given in (
4.47
).
These observations establish
the desired supersolution properties for the
function in (
4.49
) for
0<\gamma\leq 1
.
From this and the comparison result
in Lemma
4.9
, used here with
w(t)
and
v(t):=\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}
, we obtain that

v(t)\leq w(t)
for any
t\geq 0
, and in particular,

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}\leq Ke^{-\frac{t}{C}}\qquad{\mbox{for any\penalty\ $t>t_{0}$}}

(4.50)

for
K:=\theta_{0}e^{\frac{t_{0}}{C}}
.
This proves (
4.6
).

Now we deal with the case
\gamma>1
. In this case, we set

w_{0}:=\max\left\{\|u_{0}(\cdot)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)},\Big(\frac{C}{\gamma-1}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}\right\}.

Then the function

w(t):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}w_{0},&{\mbox{if }}t\in[0,1]\\
w_{0}t^{-\frac{1}{\gamma-1}},&{\mbox{if }}t>1\end{array}\right.

(4.51)

is a supersolution of (
4.44
). Indeed, if
t>1
,

\displaystyle C\left(w^{\prime}(t)+\frac{1}{C}w^{\gamma}(t)\right)=-\frac{C}{\gamma-1}w_{0}t^{-\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}}+w_{0}^{\gamma}t^{-\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}}=w_{0}t^{-\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}}\left(w_{0}^{1-\gamma}-\frac{C}{\gamma-1}\right)\geq 0,

while, if
t\in(0,1)
,

w^{\prime}(t)+\frac{1}{C}w^{\gamma}(t)=\frac{1}{C}w^{\gamma}(t)\geq 0.

This gives that the function in (
4.51
) has the desired supersolution property and consequently we can apply the comparison result
in Lemma
4.9
with
w(t)
and
v(t):=\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}
. In this way, we obtain that for all
t\geq 1

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}\leq w_{0}t^{-\frac{1}{\gamma-1}},

and so
the proof of (
4.7
)
is complete.
∎

Now, we present the applications of the abstract results to the operators introduced in Section
4.1.4
.

We start with the case of the fractional porous medium equation.

Proof of Theorem
4.3
.

In order to prove Theorem
4.3
, our strategy is to verify the
validity of inequality (
4.2
) with
\gamma:=2

for the porous medium operator,
which would put us in the position of exploiting Theorems
4.1

and
4.2
.

To this end, by elementary computations, up to changes of the positive constant
c
depending on
n,\ s,
and
\sigma
, we see that

\begin{split}\int_{\Omega}u^{s-1}(x,t)\mathcal{N}[u](x,t)\;dx&=\int_{\Omega}-u^{s-1}\nabla\cdot(u\nabla\mathcal{K}u)(x,t)\;dx\hskip 100.00015pt\\
&=\int_{\Omega}(s-1)u^{s-1}(x,t)\nabla u(x,t)\cdot\nabla\mathcal{K}u(x,t)dx\\
&=\int_{\Omega}\nabla u^{s}(x,t)\cdot\nabla\mathcal{K}u(x,t)\,dx\end{split}

(4.52)

Now, define for
\varepsilon>0
, the regularized operator

\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}u=\int_{\Omega}c(n,\sigma)\frac{u(x-y,t)}{(|y|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{n-2\sigma}{2}}}dy.

(4.53)

where
c(n,\sigma)
is the same constant that appears in the definition of
\mathcal{K}
in (
4.8
).
Notice that, since
u
is regular, we have

\int_{\Omega}\nabla u^{s}(x,t)\cdot\nabla\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}u(x,t)\,dx\\
\leq\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{\chi_{\Omega}(x)\underset{x\in\Omega}{\sup}|\nabla u^{s}(x,t)|\,\chi_{\Omega}(x-y)\underset{(x-y)\in\Omega}{\sup}|\nabla u(x-y,t)|}{|y|^{n-2\sigma}}dxdy

(4.54)

where
\chi
is the characteristic function. Thus, thanks to (
4.54
) we can apply the
Dominated Convergence Theorem and obtain

\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\int_{\Omega}\nabla u^{s}(x,t)\cdot\nabla\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}u(x,t)\,dx=\int_{\Omega}\nabla u^{s}(x,t)\cdot\nabla\mathcal{K}u(x,t)\,dx.

(4.55)

So, using (
4.52
) and (
4.55
), we have

\begin{split}\int_{\Omega}u^{s-1}(x,t)\mathcal{N}[u](x,t)\;dx&=\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\int_{\Omega}\nabla u^{s}(x,t)\cdot\nabla\mathcal{K}_{\varepsilon}u(x,t)\,dx\\
&=\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\int_{\Omega}\nabla u^{s}(x,t)\cdot\int_{\Omega}\frac{(-n+2\sigma)c(n,\sigma)u(y)}{(|x-y|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{n-2\sigma+2}{2}}}(x-y)dy\,dx\\
&=\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\lim}\iint_{\Omega\times\Omega}\dfrac{c(n,\sigma)u(y,t)\nabla u^{s}(x,t)\cdot(y-x)}{(|x-y|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{n-2\sigma+2}{2}}}\;dy\,dx,\end{split}

(4.56)

up to changes of the positive constant
c(n,\sigma)
. Now we adapt a method that was introduced in
[
29
]
to obtain
L^{p}
estimates. We exchange the order of integration and have that

\begin{split}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}c\,u(y,t)\dfrac{\nabla u^{s}(x,t)\cdot(y-x)}{(|x-y|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{n-2\sigma+2}{2}}}\;dx\,dy\\
&\hskip-120.00018pt=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}c\,u(y,t)\dfrac{\nabla(u^{s}(x,t)-u^{s}(y,t))\cdot(y-x)}{(|x-y|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{n-2\sigma+2}{2}}}\;dx\,dy\\
&\hskip-120.00018pt=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}-c{(u^{s}(x,t)-u^{s}(y,t))u(y,t)}\Bigg[\dfrac{-n}{(|x-y|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{n-2\sigma+2}{2}}}\\
&\hskip-90.00014pt+\frac{(n-2\sigma+2)|x-y|^{2}}{(|x-y|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{n-2\sigma+4}{2}}}\Bigg]dx\,dy\\
&\hskip-120.00018pt=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}c\frac{(u^{s}(x,t)-u^{s}(y,t))(u(x,t)-u(y,t))}{2}\Bigg[\dfrac{-n}{(|x-y|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{n-2\sigma+2}{2}}}\\
&\hskip-90.00014pt+\frac{(n-2\sigma+2)|x-y|^{2}}{(|x-y|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{n-2\sigma+4}{2}}}\Bigg]dx\,dy.\end{split}

We observe now that, since
(u^{s}(x,t)-u^{s}(y,t))(u(x,t)-u(y,t))
is always positive,

\begin{split}&\hskip-30.00005pt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}c\frac{(u^{s}(x,t)-u^{s}(y,t))(u(x,t)-u(y,t))}{2}\Bigg[\dfrac{-n}{(|x-y|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{n-2\sigma+2}{2}}}\\
&+\frac{(n-2\sigma+2)|x-y|^{2}}{(|x-y|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2})^{\frac{n-2\sigma+4}{2}}}\Bigg]dx\,dy\\
&\leq\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}c\frac{(u^{s}(x,t)-u^{s}(y,t))(u(x,t)-u(y,t))(2-2\sigma)}{2|x-y|^{n+2(1-\sigma)}}dx\,dy.\end{split}

Thus, again by the Dominated Convergence Theorem, we can pass to the limit in (
4.56
) and obtain

\begin{split}&\hskip-15.00002pt\int_{\Omega}u^{s-1}(x,t)\mathcal{N}[u](x,t)\;dx\\
&\hskip 15.00002pt=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}c\frac{(u^{s}(x,t)-u^{s}(y,t))(u(x,t)-u(y,t))(2-2\sigma)}{2|x-y|^{n+2(1-\sigma)}}dx\,dy.\end{split}

(4.57)

Now, we define
v(x,t)=u^{\frac{s+1}{2}}(x,t)
. Then, by inequality (2.15) of
[
43
]
we have, for some
C>0
,

C(u^{s}(x,t)-u^{s}(y,t))(u(x,t)-u(y,t))\geq|v(x,t)-v(y,t)|^{2}.

From this, (
4.52
) and (
4.57
) we obtain that

\begin{split}&C\int_{\Omega}u^{s-1}(x,t)\mathcal{N}[u](x,t)\;dx\\
&\qquad=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}c\,C\,\dfrac{2-2s}{2}\dfrac{(u^{s}(x,t)-u^{s}(y,t))(u(x,t)-u(y,t))}{|x-y|^{n+2(1-\sigma)}}\;dx\,dy\\
&\qquad\geq\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}c\,\dfrac{2-2s}{2}\dfrac{|v(x,t)-v(y,t)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{n+2(1-\sigma)}}\;dx\,dy.\end{split}

(4.58)

Now we set
z:=(1-s)
; then
z\in(0,1)
and
n\geq 2z
. Let also

p_{z}:=\dfrac{2n}{n-2z}\geq 2.

Then for any
q\in[2,p_{z}]
we can apply the Gagliardo-Sobolev-Slobodetskiĭ fractionary inequality (compare
[
40
]
, Theorem 6.5) and obtain

\left(\int_{\Omega}u^{\frac{s+1}{2}q}\right)^{\frac{2}{q}}=\|v\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq C\iint\dfrac{|v(x,t)-v(y,t)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{n+2z}}\;dxdy

(4.59)

with
C
depending only on
\Omega,\ n,\ z
and
q
.
In particular, choosing
q=2
, we deduce from (
4.59
) that

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s+1}(\Omega)}^{s+1}\leq C\iint\dfrac{|v(x,t)-v(y,t)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{n+2z}}\;dxdy

(4.60)

On the other hand, using the Hölder inequality, one has that

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}^{s+1}\leq\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s+1}(\Omega)}^{s+1}|\Omega|^{1/s}.

Combining this and (
4.60
), we obtain

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s+1}(\Omega)}^{s}\leq C\iint\dfrac{|v(x,t)-v(y,t)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{n+2z}}\;dxdy,

up to renaming
C>0
.
This and (
4.58
) establish the validity
of (
4.2
) for
\gamma:=2
, as desired.
∎

Now we focus on the Kirchhoff equation, first dealing with the case
of classical derivatives.

Proof of Theorem
4.4
.

Our objective here is to verify the
validity of inequality (
4.2
) for suitable values
of
\gamma
, and then make use of Theorems
4.1

and
4.2
.

First we present the proof for the non-degenerate case, that takes place when
m(\xi)
has a positive minimum. Let us call
m_{0}:=\min m(\xi)
, then

m\left(\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\right)\int_{\Omega}|u|^{s-2}u(-\Delta)u\;dx\geq m_{0}\int_{\Omega}|u|^{s-2}u(-\Delta)u\;dx.

(4.61)

In Theorem 1.2 of
[
43
]
, the case of the Laplacian was considered:
there it was found that, for some
C>0
depending on
s,\ n,\ \Omega
,

\int_{\Omega}|u|^{s-2}u(-\Delta)u\;dx\geq C\|u\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}^{s}.

Combining this with (
4.61
)
we see that (
4.2
) holds true for
\gamma=1
and
C>0
depending on
s,\ n,\ \Omega,\ \min m(\xi)
.

Now we deal with
the degenerate case, which requires the use of
finer estimates. In this case, we have that

\begin{split}&\hskip-30.00005ptb\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\int_{\Omega}|u(x,t)|^{s-2}u(x,t)(-\Delta)u(x,t)\;dx\\
&\hskip 30.00005pt=b\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\int_{\Omega}|u(x,t)|^{s-2}|\nabla u(x,t)|^{2}\;dx\\
&\hskip 30.00005pt\geq C\left(\int_{\Omega}|u(x,t)|^{\frac{s-2}{2}}|\nabla u(x,t)|^{2}\;dx\right)^{2},\end{split}

(4.62)

where the first passage is an integration by parts and the last inequality holds in view of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

Now define

v(x,t):=|u|^{\frac{s+2}{4}}(x,t).

(4.63)

We have that

|\nabla v|^{2}=\left(\frac{s+2}{4}\right)^{2}|u|^{\frac{s-2}{2}}|\nabla u|^{2}.

This and (
4.62
) give that

\begin{split}&\left(\frac{s+2}{4}\right)^{4}b\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\int_{\Omega}|u(x,t)|^{s-2}u(x,t)(-\Delta)u(x,t)\;dx\\
\geq\,&C\left(\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{s+2}{4}\right)^{2}|u(x,t)|^{\frac{s-2}{2}}|\nabla u(x,t)|^{2}\;dx\right)^{2}\\
=\,&C\left(\int_{\Omega}|\nabla v(x,t)|^{2}\;dx\right)^{2}.\end{split}

(4.64)

We now use Sobolev injections (in the form
given, for instance, in formula (2.9) of
[
43
]
), remembering that
v
is zero outside
\Omega
. The inequality

\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\geq C\|v\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}

(4.65)

holds

for all
q\geq 1
if
n\in\{1,2\}
,
and for all
q\in\left[1,\displaystyle\frac{2n}{n-2}\right]
if
n>2
.

(4.66)

Therefore, we set

q:=\frac{4s}{s+2}.

(4.67)

Recalling the ranges of
s
in claim (iii) of Theorem
4.4
,
when
n>2
we have that

(n-2)q-2n=\frac{4s(n-2)}{s+2}-2n=\frac{2}{s+2}\,\big((n-4)s-2n\big)\leq 0,

which shows that the definition in (
4.67
)
fulfills the conditions in (
4.66
), and so (
4.65
)
is valid in this setting.

Hence, making use of (
4.63
), (
4.64
) and (
4.65
), up to renaming
C
line after line, we deduce that

\displaystyle b\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\int_{\Omega}|u(x,t)|^{s-2}u(x,t)(-\Delta)u(x,t)\;dx

\displaystyle\qquad\geq C\|\nabla v(\cdot,t)\|^{4}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\geq C\|v\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}^{4}=C\|u\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}^{{s+2}}.

These observations imply that
condition (
4.2
) is satisfied here
with
\gamma=3
and
C
depending on
s,\ m(\xi)
and
\Omega
.
∎

Now we deal with the case of the fractional Kirchhoff equation.

Proof of Theorem
4.5
.

As in the case of classical space-derivatives
dealt with in the proof of
Theorem
4.4
,
a quick proof for the non-degenerate case is available. Indeed,

\int_{\Omega}|u|^{s-2}u\mathcal{N}[u]\,dx=m\left(\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\right)\int_{\Omega}|u|^{s-2}u(-\Delta)^{\sigma}u\,dx\geq\int_{\Omega}m_{0}|u|^{s-2}u(-\Delta)^{\sigma}u\,dx

and in
[
43
]
it was shown that

\int_{\Omega}m_{0}|u|^{s-2}u(-\Delta)^{\sigma}u\,dx\geq\|u\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}^{s}.

Thus, the validity of inequality (
4.2
) with
\gamma=1

is established in this case.

We now deal with the degenerate case.
We fix

p\in[2,+\infty)

(4.68)

and we define

r:=\frac{s+2}{2p}\qquad{\mbox{and}}\qquad v(x,t):=|u(x,t)|^{r}.

(4.69)

We claim that

\begin{split}&|v(x,t)-v(y,t)|^{p}\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt\leq c_{0}|u(x,t)-u(y,t)|\sqrt{(u(x,t)-u(y,t))(|u(x,t)|^{s-2}u(x,t)-|u(y,t)|^{s-2}u(y,t))}\end{split}

(4.70)

for some
c_{0}>0
, independent of
u
. To prove this, we first observe
that the radicand in (
4.70
) is well defined, since, for every
a
,
b\in\mathbb{R}
we have that

(a-b)(|a|^{s-2}a-|b|^{s-2}b)\geq 0.

(4.71)

To check this, up to exchanging
a
and
b
, we can suppose that
a\geq b
.
Then, we have three cases to take into account: either
a\geq b\geq 0
,
or
a\geq 0\geq b
, or
0\geq a\geq b
.
If
a\geq b\geq 0
, we have that

|a|^{s-2}a-|b|^{s-2}b=a^{s-1}-b^{s-1}\geq 0,

and so (
4.71
) holds true. If instead
a\geq 0\geq b
, we have that

|a|^{s-2}a-|b|^{s-2}b=|a|^{s-1}+|b|^{s-1}\geq 0,

which gives (
4.71
)
in this case. Finally, if
0\geq a\geq b
,

|a|^{s-2}a-|b|^{s-2}b=-|a|^{s-1}+|b|^{s-1}\geq 0,

again since
-|a|=a\geq b=-|b|
, thus completing the proof of (
4.71
).

Then, by (
4.71
), we have that (
4.70
)
is equivalent to

|v(x,t)-v(y,t)|^{2p}\leq c_{1}(u(x,t)-u(y,t))^{3}{(|u(x,t)|^{s-2}u(x,t)-|u(y,t)|^{s-2}u(y,t))}.

(4.72)

We also note that when
u(x,t)=u(y,t)
the inequality in (
4.72
)
is trivially satisfied.
Hence, without loss of generality we can suppose that

|u(x,t)|>|u(y,t)|
,  for fixed
x,\ y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

(4.73)

We define
the function

(-1,1)\ni\lambda\mapsto g(\lambda)=\frac{(1-|\lambda|^{\frac{s+2}{2p}})^{2p}}{(1-\lambda)^{3}(1-|\lambda|^{s-2}\lambda)}

(4.74)

and we claim that

\sup_{(-1,1)}g(\lambda)<+\infty.

(4.75)

To this end, we point out that

g
is regular
for all
\lambda\in(-1,1)
, so, to establish (
4.75
), we only have to study the
limits of
g
for
\lambda\rightarrow-1^{+}
and
\lambda\rightarrow 1^{-}
.

When
\lambda\rightarrow-1^{+}
, this limit is immediate and
g(-1)=0
. On the other hand,
when
\lambda\rightarrow 1^{-}
, we see that

\begin{split}\underset{\lambda\rightarrow 1^{-}}{\lim}g(\lambda)&=\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}{\lim}\frac{(1-(1-\varepsilon)^{\frac{s+2}{2p}})^{2p}}{(1-(1-\varepsilon))^{3}(1-(1-\varepsilon)^{s-1})}\\
&=\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}{\lim}\frac{\left(\frac{s+2}{2p}\varepsilon+O(\varepsilon^{2})\right)^{2p}}{\varepsilon^{3}((s-1)\varepsilon+O(\varepsilon^{2}))}\\
&=\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}}{\lim}\frac{\varepsilon^{2p-4}\,\left(\frac{s+2}{2p}+O(\varepsilon)\right)^{2p}}{(s-1+O(\varepsilon))},\end{split}

which is finite, thanks to (
4.68
).
Then (
4.75
) holds true, as desired.

Then, using (
4.75
) with
\lambda:=\frac{b}{a}
, we have that

\begin{split}&{\mbox{for any\penalty\ $a$, $b\in\mathbb{R}$ with $|a|>|b|$,}}\\
&\frac{\left(\left|a\right|^{\frac{s+2}{2p}}-\left|b\right|^{\frac{s+2}{2p}}\right)^{2p}}{\left(a-b\right)^{3}\left(\left|a\right|^{s-2}a-\left|b\right|^{s-2}b\right)}\;=\;\frac{|a|^{s+2}}{|a|^{s-2}\;a^{4}}\cdot\frac{\left(1-\left|\frac{b}{a}\right|^{\frac{s+2}{2p}}\right)^{2p}}{\left(1-\frac{b}{a}\right)^{3}\left(1-\left|\frac{b}{a}\right|^{s-2}\frac{b}{a}\right)}\\
&\qquad\;=\;\frac{(1-|\lambda|^{\frac{s+2}{2p}})^{2p}}{(1-\lambda)^{3}(1-|\lambda|^{s-2}\lambda)}\;=\;g(\lambda)\leq C,\end{split}

(4.76)

for some
C>0
. Then, in view of (
4.73
),
we can exploit (
4.76
)
with
a:=u(x,t)
and
b:=u(y,t)
, from which we obtain that

\displaystyle\left|\left|u(x,t)\right|^{\frac{s+2}{2p}}-\left|u(y,t)\right|^{\frac{s+2}{2p}}\right|^{2p}\;=\;\left(\left|u(x,t)\right|^{\frac{s+2}{2p}}-\left|u(y,t)\right|^{\frac{s+2}{2p}}\right)^{2p}

\displaystyle\qquad\;\leq\;C\,\left(u(x,t)-u(y,t)\right)^{3}\left(\left|u(x,t)\right|^{s-2}u(x,t)-\left|u(y,t)\right|^{s-2}u(y,t)\right).

This and (
4.69
)
imply (
4.70
), as desired.

Now, fixed

p
as in (
4.68
),
we set

z:=\frac{2\sigma}{p}\in(0,\sigma]\subset(0,1).

(4.77)

We apply the Gagliardo-Sobolev-Slobodetskiĭ fractional immersion (for instance, in the version given
in formula (2.18) of
[
43
]
) to
v
.
In this way,

for all
q\in[1,+\infty)
when
n\leq zp
, and for all
q\in\left[1,\displaystyle\dfrac{np}{n-zp}\right]
when
n>zp
,

(4.78)

we have that

\begin{split}\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\frac{(s+2)q}{2p}}(\Omega)}^{\frac{s+2}{2}}=\|v(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}^{p}&\,\leq C\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\frac{|v(x,t)-v(y,t)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{n+zp}}dxdy\\
&=C\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\frac{|v(x,t)-v(y,t)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{n+2\sigma}}dxdy,\end{split}

(4.79)

where the first equality comes from (
4.69
) and the
latter equality is a consequence of (
4.77
).

Now we choose

p:=\max\left\{2,\,\frac{s+2}{2}\right\}\qquad{\mbox{and}}\qquad q:=\frac{2sp}{s+2}.

(4.80)

Notice that condition (
4.68
) is fulfilled in this setting.
Furthermore, recalling (
4.77
) and
the assumptions in point (iii)
of Theorem
4.5
, we have that, when
n>2\sigma=zp
,
we have

\displaystyle(n-zp)q-np=\frac{2(n-2\sigma)sp}{s+2}-np=\frac{p}{s+2}\,\big(2(n-2\sigma)s-n(s+2)\big)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{p}{s+2}\,\big(s(n-4\sigma)-2n\big)\leq 0.

As a consequence, we have that condition (
4.78
)
is fulfilled the setting prescribed by (
4.80
),
hence we can exploit (
4.79
) in this framework.

Then, from (
4.80
) we have that

\frac{(s+2)q}{2p}=s,

and so (
4.79
) gives that

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}^{\frac{s+2}{2}}\leq C\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\frac{|v(x,t)-v(y,t)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{n+2\sigma}}dxdy.

Hence, recalling (
4.70
), up to renaming
C>0
,
we have that

\begin{split}&\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}^{s+2}\\
\leq\,&C\big(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\frac{|u(x,t)-u(y,t)|\sqrt{(u(x,t)-u(y,t))}}{|x-y|^{n+2\sigma}}\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt\times(|u(x,t)|^{s-2}u(x,t)-|u(y,t)|^{s-2}u(y,t))dxdy\big)^{2}\\
\leq\,&C\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\frac{|u(x,t)-u(y,t)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{n+2\sigma}}dxdy\\
&\times\hskip 20.00003pt\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\frac{{(u(x,t)-u(y,t))(|u(x,t)|^{s-2}u(x,t)-|u(y,t)|^{s-2}u(y,t))}}{|x-y|^{n+2\sigma}}dxdy.\end{split}

(4.81)

Notice also that, in the degenerate case, we deduce from (
4.12
)
and (
4.13
) that

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}{\mathcal{N}}[u](x,t)\,|u(x,t)|^{s-2}\,u(x,t)\,dx\\
=\;&-M_{u}\,\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\Big(u(x+y,t)+u(x-y,t)-2u(x,t)\Big)\,|u(x,t)|^{s-2}\,u(x,t)\,\frac{dx\,dy}{|y|^{n+2\sigma}}\\
=\;&-2M_{u}\,\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\big(u(y,t)-u(x,t)\big)\,|u(x,t)|^{s-2}\,u(x,t)\,\frac{dx\,dy}{|x-y|^{n+2\sigma}}\\
=\;&2M_{u}\,\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\big(u(x,t)-u(y,t)\big)\,|u(x,t)|^{s-2}\,u(x,t)\,\frac{dx\,dy}{|x-y|^{n+2\sigma}}\\
=\;&M_{u}\,\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\big(u(x,t)-u(y,t)\big)\,\big(|u(x,t)|^{s-2}\,u(x,t)-|u(y,t)|^{s-2}\,u(y,t)\big)\,\frac{dx\,dy}{|x-y|^{n+2\sigma}},\end{split}

(4.82)

with

\begin{split}M_{u}\,&:=M\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\frac{|u(x,t)-u(y,t)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{n+2\sigma}}\,dx\,dy\right)\\
&\geq b\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\frac{|u(x,t)-u(y,t)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{n+2\sigma}}\,dx\,dy,\end{split}

(4.83)

with
b>0
.

Then, from (
4.82
)
and (
4.83
),

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}{\mathcal{N}}[u](x,t)\,|u(x,t)|^{s-2}\,u(x,t)\,dx\geq b\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\frac{|u(x,t)-u(y,t)|^{2}}{|x-y|^{n+2\sigma}}\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle\times\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\big(u(x,t)-u(y,t)\big)\,\big(|u(x,t)|^{s-2}\,u(x,t)-|u(y,t)|^{s-2}\,u(y,t)\big)\,\frac{dx\,dy}{|x-y|^{n+2\sigma}}.

Comparing this with (
4.81
), we conclude that

\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}^{s+2}\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}{\mathcal{N}}[u](x,t)\,|u(x,t)|^{s-2}\,u(x,t)\,dx,

up to renaming
C
.
This gives that hypothesis (
4.2
) is fulfilled
in this case with
\gamma=3
.
∎

Now we deal with the case of the magnetic operators.
We start with the case of classical space-derivatives.
For this, we exploit an elementary, but useful, inequality,
stated in the following auxiliary result:

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
a
,
b\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\alpha
,
\beta
,
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Then

(a^{2}+b^{2})\Big(|at-\beta|^{2}+|bt+\alpha|^{2}\Big)\geq|a\alpha+b\beta|^{2}.

(4.84)

Proof.

For any
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we define

f(t):=(a^{2}+b^{2})\Big(|at-\beta|^{2}+|bt+\alpha|^{2}\Big)-|a\alpha+b\beta|^{2}.

(4.85)

We observe that

\begin{split}f(0)\,&=(a^{2}+b^{2})(\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2})-|a\alpha+b\beta|^{2}\\
&=a^{2}\alpha^{2}+a^{2}\beta^{2}+b^{2}\alpha^{2}+b^{2}\beta^{2}-(a^{2}\alpha^{2}+b^{2}\beta^{2}+2ab\alpha\beta)\\
&=a^{2}\beta^{2}+b^{2}\alpha^{2}-2ab\alpha\beta\\
&=|a\beta-b\alpha|^{2}.\end{split}

(4.86)

Moreover

\lim_{|t|\to+\infty}f(t)=\left\{\begin{matrix}+\infty&{\mbox{ if }}a^{2}+b^{2}>0,\\
0&{\mbox{ otherwise.}}\end{matrix}\right.

(4.87)

Now we claim that

f(t)\geq 0,

(4.88)

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. To prove (
4.88
) we argue by contradiction
and assume that

\inf_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f<0.

Then, in view of (
4.86
) and (
4.87
), we have that

f(\bar{t})=\inf_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f<0,

(4.89)

for some
\bar{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. As a consequence,

0=\nabla f(\bar{t})=2(a^{2}+b^{2})\Big(a(a\bar{t}-\beta)+b(b\bar{t}+\alpha)\Big)=2(a^{2}+b^{2})\Big((a^{2}+b^{2})\bar{t}-a\beta+b\alpha\Big),

which implies that

\bar{t}=\frac{a\beta-b\alpha}{a^{2}+b^{2}}.

Thus, we substitute this information into (
4.85
)
and we obtain that

\displaystyle f(\bar{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(a^{2}+b^{2})\left(\left|\frac{a^{2}\beta-ab\alpha}{a^{2}+b^{2}}-\beta\right|^{2}+\left|\frac{ab\beta-b^{2}\alpha}{a^{2}+b^{2}}+\alpha\right|^{2}\right)-|a\alpha+b\beta|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(a^{2}+b^{2})\left(\left|\frac{b^{2}\beta+ab\alpha}{a^{2}+b^{2}}\right|^{2}+\left|\frac{ab\beta+a^{2}\alpha}{a^{2}+b^{2}}\right|^{2}\right)-|a\alpha+b\beta|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(a^{2}+b^{2})\left(b^{2}\left|\frac{b\beta+a\alpha}{a^{2}+b^{2}}\right|^{2}+a^{2}\left|\frac{b\beta+a\alpha}{a^{2}+b^{2}}\right|^{2}\right)-|a\alpha+b\beta|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(a^{2}+b^{2})(a^{2}+b^{2})\left|\frac{b\beta+a\alpha}{a^{2}+b^{2}}\right|^{2}-|a\alpha+b\beta|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

This is in contradiction with (
4.89
) and so it proves (
4.88
),
which in turn implies (
4.84
), as desired.
∎

With this, we are now in the position of completing the proof
of Theorem
4.6
and obtain the desired decay estimates
for the classical magnetic operator.

Proof of Theorem
4.6
.

We want to prove inequality (
4.2
) for the classical magnetic operator in order to apply Theorem
4.1
.
To this end,
we aim at proving that

\Re\big\{\bar{u}{\mathcal{N}}u\big\}+|u|\Delta|u|\geq 0.

(4.90)

To check this, we observe
5
5

5

For an alternative proof based
on fractional arguments, see the forthcoming footnote
6
.

that we can make the computations
in the vicinity of a point
x
for which
|u(x)|>0
.
Indeed, if (
4.90
) holds true at
\{|u|>0\}
,
we can fix
\epsilon>0
and consider the function
u_{\epsilon}:=u+\epsilon
.
In this way,
u_{\epsilon}(x)=\epsilon>0
, hence we can apply (
4.90
)
to
u_{\epsilon}
and conclude that

\begin{split}0\,&\leq\Re\big\{\bar{u}_{\epsilon}(x){\mathcal{N}}u_{\epsilon}(x)\big\}+|u_{\epsilon}(x)|\Delta|u_{\epsilon}(x)|\\
&=\Re\big\{(\bar{u}(x)+\epsilon){\mathcal{N}}u(x)\big\}+|u(x)+\epsilon|\Delta|u_{\epsilon}(x)|.\end{split}

(4.91)

Notice that, for any test function
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)
,
we have that

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{\Omega}\Delta|u_{\epsilon}(y)|\,\varphi(y)\,dy=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{\Omega}|u_{\epsilon}(y)|\,\Delta\varphi(y)\,dy=\int_{\Omega}|u(y)|\,\Delta\varphi(y)\,dy,

and so (in the distributional sense)

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\Delta|u_{\epsilon}|=\Delta|u|.

Hence, we can pass to the limit in (
4.91
)
and obtain (
4.90
).

Accordingly, to prove (
4.90
), from now on we will
focus on the case in which
|u|>0
. We write
u=a+ib
and
we observe that

\begin{split}&\Re\{-\bar{u}(\nabla-iA)^{2}u\}\\
=\;&\Re\left\{-\bar{u}(\Delta u-|A|^{2}u-iA\cdot\nabla u-\nabla\cdot(iAu))\right\}\\
=\;&\Re\left\{-\bar{u}\Delta u+|A|^{2}|u|^{2}+2\bar{u}iA\cdot\nabla u+i(\nabla\cdot A)|u|^{2}\right\}\\
=\;&\Re\left\{(-a+ib)(\Delta a+i\Delta b)+|A|^{2}(a^{2}+b^{2})+2(b+ia)A\cdot(\nabla a+i\nabla b)+i(\nabla\cdot A)|u|^{2}\right\}\\
=\;&-a\Delta a-b\Delta b+|A|^{2}(a^{2}+b^{2})+2b\nabla a\cdot A-2a\nabla b\cdot A,\end{split}

(4.92)

where we used the fact that
A
is
real valued.

On the other hand, at points where
|u|\neq 0
,

\displaystyle\Delta|u|^{2}=2|u|\Delta|u|+2|\nabla|u||^{2}

and

\displaystyle\nabla|u|=\frac{a\nabla a+b\nabla b}{|u|},

therefore

\displaystyle|u|\Delta|u|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,\Delta|u|^{2}-|\nabla|u||^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,\Delta(a^{2}+b^{2})-\frac{|a\nabla a+b\nabla b|^{2}}{|u|^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a\Delta a+b\Delta b+|\nabla a|^{2}+|\nabla b|^{2}-\frac{|a\nabla a+b\nabla b|^{2}}{a^{2}+b^{2}}.

From this and (
4.92
), we conclude that

\begin{split}&\Re\big\{\bar{u}{\mathcal{N}}u\big\}+|u|\Delta|u|\\
=\;&|\nabla a|^{2}+|\nabla b|^{2}-\frac{|a\nabla a+b\nabla b|^{2}}{a^{2}+b^{2}}+|A|^{2}(a^{2}+b^{2})+2b\nabla a\cdot A-2a\nabla b\cdot A\\
=\;&\big|aA-\nabla b\big|^{2}+\big|bA+\nabla a\big|^{2}-\frac{|a\nabla a+b\nabla b|^{2}}{a^{2}+b^{2}},\end{split}

(4.93)

and the latter term is nonnegative, thanks to (
4.84
)
(applied here with
t:=A
,
\alpha:=\nabla a
and
\beta:=\nabla b
).
This completes the proof of (
4.90
).

Then, from (
4.90
) here and
[
43
]

(see in particular the formula before (2.12) in
[
43
]
,
exploited here with
p:=2
and
m:=2
),

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u|^{s-2}\Re\big\{\bar{u}{\mathcal{N}}u\big\}\,dx\geq-\int_{\Omega}|u|^{s-1}\Delta|u|\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{\Omega}\nabla|u|^{s-1}\cdot\nabla|u|\,dx\geq C\,\|u\|_{L^{s}{(\Omega)}}^{s},

for some
C>0
.
This establishes inequality (
4.2
) in this case,
with
\gamma=1
. Hence, Theorem
4.6
follows
from Theorems
4.1
and
4.2
.
∎

Now we deal with the fractional magnetic operator.

Proof of Theorem
4.7
.

We have to verify the structural hypothesis (
4.2
). We already know
that the desired inequality holds for the fractional Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{\sigma}v
for
\sigma\in(0,1)
and
v\geq 0
(compare Theorem 1.2 of
[
43
]
). We notice that

\begin{split}\Re&\left\{\frac{\bar{u}(x,t)\left(u(x,t)-e^{i(x-y)A(\frac{x+y}{2})}u(y,t)\right)}{|x-y|^{n+2\sigma}}\right\}\\
&\hskip 30.00005pt=\frac{|{u}(x,t)|^{2}-\Re\left\{e^{i(x-y)A(\frac{x+y}{2})}u(y,t)\bar{u}(x,t)\right\}}{|x-y|^{n+2\sigma}}\\
&\hskip 30.00005pt\geq|u(x,t)|\frac{|{u}(x,t)|-|u(y,t)|}{|x-y|^{n+2\sigma}},\end{split}

(4.94)

and therefore
6
6

6

Interestingly,
integrating and taking the limit as
\sigma\to 1
in (
4.94
),
one obtains an alternative (and conceptually simpler)
proof of (
4.90
).
This is a nice example of analysis in a nonlocal setting
which carries useful information to the classical case.

\int_{\Omega}|u(x,t)|^{s-2}\Re\{\bar{u}(x,t)\mathcal{N}[u](x,t)\}\;dx\geq\int_{\Omega}|u(x,t)|^{s-1}(-\Delta)^{\sigma}|u|(x,t)\;dx.

(4.95)

Also, since
|u|
is a real and positive function, we can exploit
formula (2.25) in
[
43
]
(used here with
p:=2
) and write that

\int_{\Omega}|u(x,t)|^{s-1}(-\Delta)^{\sigma}|u|(x,t)\;dx\geq{C}\|u\|_{L^{s}(\Omega)}^{s}.

From this and (
4.95
) we infer that
condition (
4.2
) is satisfied in this case
with
\gamma=1
.
Then, the desired conclusion in Theorem
4.7

follows from Theorems
4.1

and
4.2
.
∎
